{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_2","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,240p' /root/.codex/skills/review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities/SKILL.md\"","aggregated_output":"---\nname: review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities\ndescription: >-\n  Reviews application source code for security vulnerabilities. First works out\n  what the code actually does, then searches THIS skill library for the\n  specialised skills that match the code's security-relevant behaviour, then\n  applies those skills to audit the code and reports findings with CWE ids and\n  fixes. Use this whenever asked to review, audit, or security-check source\n  code, do a secure code review, find vulnerabilities, or check code against the\n  OWASP Top 10 or CWE. Keywords: code review, secure code review, source code\n  audit, find vulnerabilities, security assessment of code, OWASP, CWE, SQL\n  injection, weak cryptography, hardcoded secrets, insecure deserialization,\n  XSS, SSRF, authentication and session flaws, JWT verification, password\n  storage.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: application-security\ntags:\n  - code-review\n  - security-review\n  - owasp\n  - cwe\n  - sast\n  - dispatcher\nversion: \"1.1\"\nlicense: Apache-2.0\n---\n# Reviewing Code for Security Vulnerabilities\n\n## Overview\n\nThis is a router skill. On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is present.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

It is safe to show\n    10\t *       to an end user or return over an API, and {@link #getMessage()} returns exactly it, so a\n    11\t *       caller who blindly renders {@code e.getMessage()} leaks nothing.</li>\n    12\t *   <li>{@link #getTechnicalDetail()} is a one-line, secret-free diagnostic intended for the log.\n    13\t *       It is never reachable from {@link #getMessage()} or {@link #toString()}.</li>\n    14\t * </ul>\n    15\t *\n    16\t * <p>Stack traces are disabled: this component has a fixed, small set of failure modes and their\n    17\t * causes are already named by {@link Crypto FailureCode}, so a stack trace would add nothing while\n    18\t * inviting callers to log internal package layout alongside it.\n    19\t *\n    20\t * <p>Cryptographic failures in particular never carry the underlying {@code\n    21\t * javax.crypto.AEADBadTagException} as a {@code cause} that survives into {@link #getMessage()};\n    22\t * the code records it, the detail string describes it, and the key material never does.\n    23\t */\n    24\tpublic final class CryptoException extends Exception {\n    25\t\n    26\t    private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;\n    27\t\n    28\t    /** Machine-readable failure category. Covers a wrong key, a tampered blob and a wrong context. */\n    41\t        AUTHENTICATION_FAILED(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n    42\t        /** Key material was supplied in a form this component refuses to accept. */\n    43\t        INVALID_KEY(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n    44\t        /** A key or password source is absent or unusable. Never contains the secret. */\n    45\t        KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n    46\t        /** A key store or key file failed its ownership or permission requirements. */\n    47\t        INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE(\"The data could not be decrypted\"),\n    48\t        /** A ciphertext exceeded {@link Limits#MAX_CIPHERTEXT_BYTES} before anything was allocated. */\n    49\t        TOO_LARGE(\"The supplied data is not in a supported format\"),\n    50\t        /** The underlying platform refused the primitive. Contains no record, key, path or existence information. */\n    60\t        public String userMessage() {\n    61\t            return userMessage;\n    62\t        }\n    63\t    }\n    64\t\n    65\t    /*\n    66\t     * Not transient. `getMessage()` returns `code.userMessage()`, so a code that did not survive\n    67\t     * deserialization would make the generic message throw a NullPointerException - on the error path,\n    68\t     * where the caller's own handler replaces the safe fixed vocabulary with an unrelated failure. The\n    69\t     * enum names only a category, so serializing it discloses nothing. `technicalDetail` stays\n    70\t     * transient: it can name a path or a component, and it is meant for the log of the JVM that made it.\n    71\t     */\n    72\t    private final CryptoFailureCode code;\n    73\t    private final transient String technicalDetail;\n    74\t\n    75\t    CryptoException(String detail) {\n    76\t        this(CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT, detail, null);\n    77\t    }\n    78\t\n    79\t    CryptoException(CryptoFailureCode code, String detail) {\n    80\t        this(code, detail, null);\n    81\t    }\n    82\t\n    83\t    CryptoException(CryptoFailureCode code, String detail, Throwable cause) {\n    84\t        super(code.userMessage(), cause, /*enableSuppression*/ false, /*writableStackTrace*/ false);\n    85\t        this.code = code;\n    86\t        this.technicalDetail = detail;\n    87\t    }\n    88\t\n    89\t    /** Failure category. */\n    90\t    public CryptoFailureCode code() {\n    91\t        return code;\n    92\t    }\n    93\t\n    94\t    /**\n    95\t     * Generic, renderable message. Deliberately identical for every failure that concerns a secret,\n    96\t     * so a response cannot be used to distinguish a wrong key from a tampered blob.\n    97\t     */\n    98\t    @Override\n    99\t    public String getMessage() {\n   100\t        return code.userMessage();\n   101\t    }\n   102\t\n   103\t    /**\n   104\t     * One-line diagnostic for logging. Never contains key bytes, passwords, plaintext, file\n   105\t     * contents, or a cause's message that might itself contain any of those.\n   106\t     */\n   107\t    public String getTechnicalDetail() {\n   108\t        return technicalDetail;\n   109\t    }\n   110\t\n   111\t    /**\n   112\t     * Writes the generic message only. The technical detail and the cause are intentionally\n   113\t     * omitted so that {@code log.error(\"...\", e)} cannot leak them through this exception's own\n   114\t     * rendering; log {@link #getTechnicalDetail()} explicitly if it is wanted.\n   115\t     */\n   116\t    @Override\n   117\t    public void printStackTrace(java.io.PrintStream ps) {\n   118\t        ps.println(getClass().getName() + \": \" + getMessage());\n   119\t    }\n   120\t\n   121\t    @Override\n   122\t    public void printStackTrace(java.io.PrintWriter pw) {\n   123\t        pw.println(getClass().getName() + \": \" + getMessage());\n   124\t    }\n   125\t\n   126\t    /**\n   127\t     * Returns a cause with its message stripped, so that generic logging of {@code getCause()}\n   128\t     * cannot surface a provider message. Callers that genuinely need the original exception should\n   129\t     * use {@link #getUnderlyingCause()} from trusted internal code.\n   130\t     */\n   131\t    @Override\n   132\t    public synchronized Throwable getCause() {\n   133\t        Throwable t = super.getCause();\n   134\t        if (t == null) {\n   135\t            return null;\n   136\t        }\n   137\t        return new ProviderFailure(t.getClass().getName());\n   138\t    }\n   139\t\n   140\t    /** Real cause, for internal diagnostics only. Never render this to an untrusted audience. */\n   141\t    public synchronized Throwable getUnderlyingCause() {\n   142\t        return super.getCause();\n   143\t    }\n   144\t\n   145\t    /**\n   146\t     * {@link #getMessage()} is final-by-override above, so this type only ever carries its own\n   147\t     * message. {@code ProviderFailure} exists to replace the wrapped cause's message with its class\n   148\t     * name; provider messages can quote key lengths, algorithm parameters and file paths.\n   149\t     */\n   150\t    private static final class ProviderFailure extends Exception {\n   151\t        private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;\n   152\t\n   153\t        ProviderFailure(String causeClassName) {\n   154\t            super(\"provider failure: \" + causeClassName, null, false, false);\n   155\t        }\n   156\t    }\n   157\t}\n===== src/main/java/com/example/crypto/EnvKeySource.java =====\n     1\tpackage com.example.crypto;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;\n     4\timport java.util.regex.Pattern;\n     5\t\n     6\t/**\n     7\t * Reads a key from an environment variable.\n     8\t *\n     9\t * <p>The name is validated before it is used, and its value is validated before it is turned into a\n    10\t * key. Environment contents are attacker-reachable in the common deployments: a compose file, a\n    11\t * {@code .env} in a world-readable checkout, {@code /proc/<pid>/environ} readable by the same uid or\n    12\t * by root, and anything that can inject into a parent shell. So an env-supplied key is parsed with the\n    13\t * same strict rules as a key file, {@link KeyEncoding}, and a malformed value is refused rather than\n    14\t * repaired.\n    15\t *\n    16\t * <p>Two things this class will not do:\n    17\t * <ul>\n    18\t *   <li>fall back to another source when the variable is unset - a missing key is an error, never a\n    19\t *       default;</li>\n    20\t *   <li>echo the value in any message. {@link #describe()} returns the variable's name, and the\n    21\t *       failure detail for a bad value reports only its length and shape, never its content.</li>\n    22\t * </ul>\n    23\t */\n    24\tpublic final class EnvKeySource implements KeySource {\n    25\t\n    26\t    /**\n    27\t     * Shape of an acceptable variable name: a shell-legal identifier. Anything else cannot be a real\n    28\t     * variable name, and refusing it stops a caller from passing attacker-chosen text (a name read\n    29\t     * from a request body, a database row or a config file) to be looked up in the process\n    30\t     * environment.\n    31\t     */\n    32\t    private static final Pattern NAME = Pattern.compile(\"[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]{0,63}\");\n    33\t\n    34\t    private static final int MAX_VALUE_BYTES = KeyEncoding.MAX_INPUT_BYTES;\n    35\t\n    36\t    private final String variable;\n    37\t\n    38\t    /**\n    39\t     * @param variable environment variable name, {@code [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]{0,63}}, supplied by\n    40\t     *     deployment configuration. It is treated as trusted only in the sense that it is operator\n    41\t     *     input; it is still validated.\n    42\t     * @throws CryptoException if the name is null, empty, over 64 characters or not an identifier\n    43\t     */\n    44\t    public EnvKeySource(String variable) throws CryptoException {\n    45\t        if (variable == null || !NAME.matcher(variable).matches()) {\n    46\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT,\n    47\t                    \"environment variable name must match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]{0,63}\");\n    48\t        }\n    49\t        this.variable = variable;\n    50\t    }\n    51\t\n    52\t    @Override\n    53\t    public KeyMaterial load() throws CryptoException {\n    54\t        String value;\n    55\t        try {\n    56\t            value = System.getenv(variable);\n    57\t        } catch (SecurityException e) {\n    58\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE,\n    59\t                    \"environment is not readable under the current security manager\", e);\n    60\t        }\n    61\t        if (value == null) {\n    62\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE,\n    63\t                    \"environment variable \" + variable + \" is not set\");\n    64\t        }\n    65\t        if (value.isEmpty()) {\n    66\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE,\n    67\t                    \"environment variable \" + variable + \" is empty\");\n    68\t        }\n    69\t        byte[] raw = value.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);\n    70\t        if (raw.length > MAX_VALUE_BYTES) {\n    71\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.TOO_LARGE,\n    72\t                    \"environment variable \" + variable + \" is larger than \" + MAX_VALUE_BYTES + \" bytes\");\n    73\t        }\n    74\t        try {\n    75\t            return KeyMaterial.fromBytes(KeyEncoding.decodeKeyBytes(raw));\n    76\t        } catch (CryptoException e) {\n    77\t            /* Prefix the source, and do not let the value survive in the detail. */\n    78\t            throw new CryptoException(e.code(), variable + \": \" + valueShape(raw), e);\n    79\t        }\n    80\t    }\n    81\t\n    82\t    /** Length and character class only - never the value. */\n    83\t    private static String valueShape(byte[] raw) {\n    84\t        boolean printable = true;\n    85\t        for (byte b : raw) {\n    86\t            int v = b & 0xFF;\n    87\t            if (v <= 0x20 || v > 0x7E) {\n    88\t                printable = false;\n    89\t                break;\n    90\t            }\n    91\t        }\n    92\t        return \"value is unusable (\" + raw.length + \" bytes, \" + (printable ? \"printable ascii\" : \"non-printable\") + \")\";\n    93\t    }\n    94\t\n    95\t    /** The variable name. Contains no value. */\n    96\t    @Override\n    97\t    public String describe() {\n    98\t        return \"env:\" + variable;\n    99\t    }\n   100\t\n   101\t    @Override\n   102\t    public boolean equals(Object o) {\n   103\t        return o instanceof EnvKeySource other && variable.equals(other.variable);\n   104\t    }\n   105\t\n   106\t    @Override\n   107\t    public int hashCode() {\n   108\t        return variable.hashCode();\n   109\t    }\n   110\t\n   111\t    @Override\n   112\t    public String toString() {\n   113\t        return describe();\n   114\t    }\n   115\t}\n===== src/main/java/com/example/crypto/KeyEncoding.java =====\n     1\tpackage com.example.crypto;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;\n     4\timport java.util.Base64;\n     5\t\n     6\t/**\n     7\t * Parses key material out of a byte buffer that came from configuration or a file.\n     8\t *\n     9\t * <p>Two shapes are accepted, and only these two:\n    10\t * <ol>\n    11\t *   <li>exactly {@value KeyMaterial#KEY_BYTES} raw bytes;</li>\n    12\t *   <li>canonical base64url text without padding, decoding to exactly\n    13\t *       {@value KeyMaterial#KEY_BYTES} bytes, with no whitespace anywhere.</li>\n    14\t * </ol>\n    15\t *\n    16\t * <p>Why the encoding rules are strict:\n    17\t * <ul>\n    18\t *   <li>{@code Base64.getMimeDecoder()} silently discards every character outside the alphabet, so\n    19\t *       {@code \"not-a-key!\"} decodes to five bytes with no error and would become a truncated,\n    20\t *       weak key. The strict decoder is used and the result is re-encoded and compared, so a\n    21\t *       non-canonical spelling is refused rather than silently accepted;</li>\n    22\t *   <li>no whitespace is accepted <em>anywhere</em>, in either form. A trailing newline is refused in\n    23\t *       the text form as well as the raw one: byte {@code 0x0A} is not printable, so it is rejected\n    24\t *       before the decoder is consulted. Trimming first is not an option - a value whose leading or\n    25\t *       trailing characters are removable is a value whose length somebody else decides, and the length\n    26\t *       is what distinguishes the raw form from the text form. The two forms are distinguishable by\n    27\t *       length alone, so there is no ambiguity to resolve and no \"try both and hope\" path;</li>\n    28\t *   <li>a buffer that is neither is rejected outright. There is no fallback that pads, truncates, or\n    29\t *       hashes the input down to 32 bytes - silently deriving a key from a malformed one is how a key\n    30\t *       rotation ends up with two different keys called the same thing.</li>\n    31\t * </ul>\n    32\t *\n    33\t * <p>There is deliberately no counterpart that renders key material as a {@code String}: a\n    34\t * {@code String} cannot be zeroed, so it would leave the key in the heap past the end of the\n    35\t * operation. {@link KeyMaterial#copyForWrite()} is the only export path and it hands back a byte array\n    36\t * the caller is expected to wipe.\n    37\t */\n    38\tpublic final class KeyEncoding {\n    39\t\n    40\t    /** Maximum size of a buffer this will look at: more than any accepted key form needs. */\n    41\t    public static final int MAX_INPUT_BYTES = 4096;\n    42\t\n    43\t    private static final Base64.Encoder CANON = Base64.getUrlEncoder().withoutPadding();\n    44\t    private static final Base64.Decoder DECODER = Base64.getUrlDecoder();\n    45\t\n    46\t    private KeyEncoding() {\n    47\t    }\n    48\t\n    49\t    /**\n    50\t     * @param raw bytes read from a key file or a configuration value; never null\n    51\t     * @return a new array of exactly {@value KeyMaterial#KEY_BYTES} bytes\n    52\t     * @throws CryptoException if the buffer is not one of the two accepted forms\n    53\t     */\n    54\t    public static byte[] decodeKeyBytes(byte[] raw) throws CryptoException {\n    55\t        byte[] in = Limits.requirePayload(raw, MAX_INPUT_BYTES, \"key material\");\n    56\t        if (in.length == KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES) {\n    57\t            return in.clone();\n    58\t        }\n    59\t        if (in.length == 0) {\n    60\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY, \"key material is empty\");\n    61\t        }\n    62\t        for (byte b : in) {\n    63\t            int v = b & 0xFF;\n    64\t            /* Control characters, including NUL and newline, are not part of the text form. */\n    65\t            if (v <= 0x20 || v > 0x7E) {\n    66\t                throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n    67\t                        \"key material is neither \" + KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES + \" raw bytes nor printable base64url text\");\n    68\t            }\n    69\t        }\n    70\t        String text = new String(in, StandardCharsets.US_ASCII);\n    71\t        byte[] decoded;\n    72\t        try {\n    73\t            decoded = DECODER.decode(text);\n    74\t        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {\n    75\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n    76\t                    \"key material is not valid base64url text\", e);\n    77\t        }\n    78\t        if (!CANON.encodeToString(decoded).equals(text)) {\n    79\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n    80\t                    \"key material is not canonically encoded base64url text\");\n    81\t        }\n    82\t        if (decoded.length != KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES) {\n    83\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n    84\t                    \"key material decodes to \" + decoded.length + \" bytes, expected \" + KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES);\n    85\t        }\n    86\t        return decoded;\n    87\t    }\n    88\t\n    89\t    /**\n    90\t     * Renders a key as canonical base64url text. For the one legitimate case: an operator-facing\n    91\t     * export used to place a key into a secret manager.\n    92\t     *\n    93\t     * <p>The result is a {@code String} and therefore <em>not</em> erasable; it exists so the caller\n    94\t     * can hand a key to a system that only accepts text. Callers must not log it, must not put it in a\n    95\t     * command line, and must not write it to a file they have not created with {@code 0600}\n    96\t     * permissions. Prefer {@code PrivateFiles.writeOwnerOnlyFile} with raw bytes.\n    97\t     *\n    98\t     * <p>{@link KeyMaterial} is required rather than a bare array so the caller has already had to\n    99\t     * hold the key in a managed object.\n   100\t     */\n   101\t    public static String toBase64Url(KeyMaterial key) throws CryptoException {\n   102\t        byte[] raw = key.copyForWrite();\n   103\t        try {\n   104\t            return CANON.encodeToString(raw);\n   105\t        } finally {\n   106\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(raw, (byte) 0);\n   107\t        }\n   108\t    }\n   109\t}\n===== src/main/java/com/example/crypto/KeyFileKeySource.java =====\n     1\tpackage com.example.crypto;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.nio.file.Path;\n     4\t\n     5\t/**\n     6\t * Reads a key from a file on disk.\n     7\t *\n     8\t * <p>The file must be a regular file, not a symlink, owned by the user running this process, with mode\n     9\t * no looser than {@code 0600}, and every ancestor directory must be owned by that user and not\n    10\t * group- or world-writable (a world-writable directory is accepted only with the sticky bit, the\n    11\t * {@code /tmp} convention). Those checks are in {@link PrivateFiles} and they are performed before the\n    12\t * file is opened, not after.\n    13\t *\n    14\t * <p>Why not simply \"read the file and let the OS sort it out\": a key file is the one input an\n    15\t * unprivileged local attacker is most likely to be able to place, rename or symlink, because the path\n    16\t * normally comes from configuration. A path from configuration is still attacker-influenceable when\n    17\t * the config is world-writable, when it lives in a shared checkout, or when the deploy pipeline can be\n    18\t * nudged - so the path is validated, and the object behind it is inspected, rather than trusted.\n    19\t *\n    20\t * <p>A trailing newline is <strong>not</strong> accepted, in either accepted form. It would have to be\n    21\t * trimmed to be, and trimming is the thing not to do here: the length is what distinguishes a raw\n    22\t * 32-byte key from base64 text, so a value whose length is decided by whatever the last byte happens to\n    23\t * be is a value whose form is attacker- or editor-decided. A key file that will not load is a deploy\n    24\t * failure to fix at the deploy step - write the file with {@link KeyMaterial#writeTo}, which produces\n    25\t * exactly 32 raw bytes and nothing else - rather than something this class guesses about. See\n    26\t * {@link KeyEncoding}.\n    27\t */\n    28\tpublic final class KeyFileKeySource implements KeySource {\n    29\t\n    30\t    private final Path file;\n    31\t\n    32\t    /**\n    33\t     * @param file path to the key file. Relative paths are resolved against the process working\n    34\t     *     directory; prefer an absolute path from deployment configuration.\n    35\t     * @throws CryptoException if {@code file} is null, has no parent, or the path is longer than 4096\n    36\t     *     characters\n    37\t     */\n    38\t    public KeyFileKeySource(Path file) throws CryptoException {\n    39\t        if (file == null) {\n    40\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.MISSING_INPUT, \"key file path is missing\");\n    41\t        }\n    42\t        String abs = file.toAbsolutePath().toString();\n    43\t        if (abs.length() > 4096) {\n    44\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT, \"key file path is too long\");\n    45\t        }\n    46\t        if (abs.indexOf('\\0') >= 0) {\n    47\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT, \"key file path is malformed\");\n    48\t        }\n    49\t        this.file = file;\n    50\t    }\n    51\t\n    52\t    @Override\n    53\t    public KeyMaterial load() throws CryptoException {\n    54\t        /*\n    55\t         * The bound here is generous (KeyEncoding rejects anything over 4 KiB anyway) because the\n    56\t         * meaningful limit is on the decoded key, and a huge file must be refused before it is read.\n    57\t         */\n    58\t        byte[] raw = PrivateFiles.readOwnerOnlyFile(file, KeyEncoding.MAX_INPUT_BYTES);\n    59\t        try {\n    60\t            return KeyMaterial.fromBytes(KeyEncoding.decodeKeyBytes(raw));\n    61\t        } finally {\n    62\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(raw, (byte) 0);\n    63\t        }\n    64\t    }\n    65\t\n    66\t    /** The path. A path is not a secret; the file's contents are. */\n    67\t    @Override\n    68\t    public String describe() {\n    69\t        return \"file:\" + file.toAbsolutePath();\n    70\t    }\n    71\t\n    72\t    @Override\n    73\t    public boolean equals(Object o) {\n    74\t        return o instanceof KeyFileKeySource other && file.equals(other.file);\n    75\t    }\n    76\t\n    77\t    @Override\n    78\t    public int hashCode() {\n    79\t        return file.hashCode();\n    80\t    }\n    81\t\n    82\t    @Override\n    83\t    public String toString() {\n    84\t        return describe();\n    85\t    }\n    86\t}\n===== src/main/java/com/example/crypto/KeyMaterial.java =====\n     1\tpackage com.example.crypto;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.util.Arrays;\n     4\t\n     5\t/**\n     6\t * An AES key held as a private, copy-owned {@code byte[]} that this class wipes as soon as it is\n     7\t * finished with.\n     8\t *\n     9\t * <p>Why not hold a {@code SecretKey}? Because {@code SecretKeySpec} keeps a live copy of the key\n    10\t * for the lifetime of the object, and its {@code destroy()} does not erase anything - on this JDK it\n    11\t * throws {@code DestroyFailedException} and leaves {@code isDestroyed()} false - so the only way to\n    12\t * actually remove the bytes from the heap is to own the array and zero it. {@code KeyMaterial} owns\n    13\t * the array.\n    14\t *\n    15\t * <p>Instances are short-lived by construction: {@link #use(java.util.function.Function)} derives a\n    16\t * {@code SecretKey} from the bytes, hands it to the callback and zeroes its own working array in a\n    17\t * {@code finally} block, and {@link #destroy()} erases the master copy. Nothing else in this component\n    18\t * is handed the key, and {@code SymmetricCrypto} never retains a {@code KeyMaterial}.\n    19\t *\n    20\t * <p>What {@code use} does <em>not</em> erase is stated rather than glossed over: {@code SecretKeySpec}\n    21\t * clones the array it is given, so the {@code Cipher} inside the callback runs on the JDK's copy, which\n    22\t * no public API reaches. Zeroing {@code working} removes this class's copy only. That JDK copy becomes\n    23\t * unreachable when the callback returns and is collected on the ordinary schedule; erasing key material\n    24\t * held inside another library is not achievable from this class, and saying otherwise would make the\n    25\t * {@code finally} block look like a control stronger than it is. It is on the residual-risk list.\n    26\t *\n    27\t * <p>Security notes that are easy to get wrong and are enforced here:\n    28\t * <ul>\n    29\t *   <li>the constructor copies the caller's array, so wiping the caller's buffer cannot corrupt\n    30\t *       this object, and this object cannot corrupt the caller's buffer;</li>\n    31\t *   <li>{@link #equals}, {@link #hashCode} and {@link #toString} never expose the bytes;</li>\n    32\t *   <li>only 256-bit AES keys are accepted. {@code SecretKeySpec} would happily construct from a\n    33\t *       20-byte array and fail later, so the length is checked here, at the boundary.</li>\n    34\t * </ul>\n    35\t */\n    36\tpublic final class KeyMaterial implements AutoCloseable {\n    37\t\n    38\t    static final int KEY_BYTES = 32;\n    39\t\n    40\t    private final int version;\n    41\t    private byte[] key;\n    42\t    private boolean destroyed;\n    43\t\n    44\t    /**\n    45\t     * Imports key bytes supplied by the caller - from a key store, an environment variable or a\n    46\t     * secret manager. The bytes are copied, so the caller may (and should) zero its own buffer\n    47\t     * immediately; exactly {@value #KEY_BYTES} bytes are accepted and anything else is refused rather\n    48\t     * than padded, truncated or hashed down. The key is version 1.\n    49\t     */\n    50\t    public static KeyMaterial fromBytes(byte[] rawKey) throws CryptoException {\n    51\t        return new KeyMaterial(rawKey, EncryptedBlob.MIN_KEY_VERSION);\n    52\t    }\n    53\t\n    54\t    /**\n    55\t     * Imports key bytes tagged with a key version, for use with {@link KeyVault}. The version is not\n    56\t     * bookkeeping metadata: it is written into every record this key encrypts and included in the\n    57\t     * AES-GCM AAD, so a record is cryptographically bound to the key version that produced it and\n    58\t     * cannot be re-stamped to claim another one.\n    59\t     *\n    60\t     * @throws CryptoException if the version is outside {@code 1..255}\n    61\t     */\n    62\t    public static KeyMaterial fromBytes(byte[] rawKey, int keyVersion) throws CryptoException {\n    63\t        if (keyVersion < EncryptedBlob.MIN_KEY_VERSION || keyVersion > EncryptedBlob.MAX_KEY_VERSION) {\n    64\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT,\n    65\t                    \"key version must be between \" + EncryptedBlob.MIN_KEY_VERSION + \" and \"\n    66\t                            + EncryptedBlob.MAX_KEY_VERSION);\n    67\t        }\n    68\t        return new KeyMaterial(rawKey, keyVersion);\n    69\t    }\n    70\t\n    71\t    /** The key version this material is tagged with. */\n    72\t    public int version() {\n    73\t        return version;\n    74\t    }\n    75\t\n    76\t    KeyMaterial(byte[] rawKey, int version) throws CryptoException {\n    77\t        this.version = version;\n    78\t        if (rawKey == null) {\n    79\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY, \"key material is missing\");\n    80\t        }\n    81\t        if (rawKey.length != KEY_BYTES) {\n    82\t            /* Security: only AES-256. A shorter key is a downgrade, not a configuration option. */\n    83\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n    84\t                    \"expected a \" + KEY_BYTES + \"-byte key, got \" + rawKey.length);\n    85\t        }\n    86\t        this.key = rawKey.clone();\n    87\t    }\n    88\t\n    89\t    /**\n    90\t     * Runs {@code action} with a {@code SecretKey} derived from this key material, then erases both\n    91\t     * copies. The action's return value is passed through.\n    92\t     *\n    93\t     * @throws CryptoException if the key has already been destroyed\n    94\t     */\n    95\t    public synchronized <T> T use(java.util.function.Function<javax.crypto.SecretKey, T> action)\n    96\t            throws CryptoException {\n    97\t        if (destroyed || key == null) {\n    98\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_NOT_ACTIVE,\n    99\t                    \"key material has already been destroyed\");\n   100\t        }\n   101\t        byte[] working = key.clone();\n   102\t        try {\n   103\t            return action.apply(new javax.crypto.spec.SecretKeySpec(working, \"AES\"));\n   104\t        } finally {\n   105\t            Arrays.fill(working, (byte) 0);\n   106\t        }\n   107\t    }\n   108\t\n   109\t    /**\n   110\t     * Package-internal raw export, used by {@link #writeTo} and {@link KeyEncoding#toBase64Url}.\n   111\t     * Deliberately not public: there is no safe generic answer to \"give me the key bytes\", so the two\n   112\t     * supported destinations - a file created private, and a canonical base64url string the caller is\n   113\t     * told not to log - are the only exits, each with its own documented handling requirements.\n   114\t     */\n   115\t    synchronized byte[] copyForWrite() throws CryptoException {\n   116\t        if (destroyed || key == null) {\n   117\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_NOT_ACTIVE,\n   118\t                    \"key material has already been destroyed\");\n   119\t        }\n   120\t        return key.clone();\n   121\t    }\n   122\t\n   123\t    /**\n   124\t     * Writes the key to a new file that is private from its first byte, for a deployment that keeps\n   125\t     * keys as files. The file is created {@code 0600} by {@link PrivateFiles} - never created readable\n   126\t     * and chmod-ed afterwards - its directory must already be private, and an existing file is refused\n   127\t     * rather than overwritten.\n   128\t     *\n   129\t     * <p>The key stays usable afterwards: this does not destroy it. Nothing about this call lets the\n   130\t     * key be recovered from the file's name, so {@link KeyFileKeySource} needs the same path to read it\n   131\t     * back.\n   132\t     *\n   133\t     * @throws CryptoException if the file exists, the directory is not private, the filesystem has no\n   134\t     *     POSIX permissions, or this material has been destroyed\n   135\t     */\n   136\t    public void writeTo(java.nio.file.Path file) throws CryptoException {\n   137\t        byte[] raw = copyForWrite();\n   138\t        try {\n   139\t            PrivateFiles.writeOwnerOnlyFile(file, raw);\n   140\t        } finally {\n   141\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(raw, (byte) 0);\n   142\t        }\n   143\t    }\n   144\t\n   145\t    /** True once the bytes have been erased. */\n   146\t    public synchronized boolean isDestroyed() {\n   147\t        return destroyed;\n   148\t    }\n   149\t\n   150\t    /** Erases the key bytes. Idempotent. */\n   151\t    @Override\n   152\t    public synchronized void close() {\n   153\t        destroy();\n   154\t    }\n   155\t\n   156\t    /** Erases the key bytes. Idempotent. */\n   157\t    public synchronized void destroy() {\n   158\t        if (key != null) {\n   159\t            Arrays.fill(key, (byte) 0);\n   160\t            key = null;\n   161\t        }\n   162\t        destroyed = true;\n   163\t    }\n   164\t\n   165\t    /**\n   166\t     * Compares by identity only. A constant-time content comparison is deliberately not offered:\n   167\t     * nothing in this component needs to compare key material, and a method that returns \"is this\n   168\t     * the same key\" is a way for a caller to mount a byte-at-a-time oracle by accident.\n   169\t     */\n   170\t    @Override\n   171\t    public boolean equals(Object o) {\n   172\t        return this == o;\n   173\t    }\n   174\t\n   175\t    @Override\n   176\t    public int hashCode() {\n   177\t        return System.identityHashCode(this);\n   178\t    }\n   179\t\n   180\t    /** Never contains key material. */\n   181\t    @Override\n   182\t    public String toString() {\n   183\t        return \"KeyMaterial[destroyed=\" + destroyed + \"]\";\n   184\t    }\n   185\t}\n===== src/main/java/com/example/crypto/KeySource.java =====\n     1\tpackage com.example.crypto;\n     2\t\n     3\t/**\n     4\t * Where a key comes from.\n     5\t *\n     6\t * <p>Every implementation loads a <em>fresh</em> {@link KeyMaterial} on each call and keeps none\n     7\t * cached. That is a deliberate cost: caching key material is the usual way a \"hardened\" component\n     8\t * ends up with the key resident in the heap for the life of the JVM, which is exactly what\n     9\t * {@link KeyMaterial#destroy()} exists to prevent. A missing, unreadable or malformed source throws\n    13\t * {@link CryptoException}; it must never return a default key, a zero key, or a key derived from a\n    14\t * constant.\n    15\t */\n    16\tpublic interface KeySource {\n    17\t\n    18\t    /**\n    19\t     * Loads key material.\n    20\t     *\n    21\t     * @throws CryptoException if the key is absent, malformed, not usable, or stored somewhere whose\n    22\t     *     privacy this component cannot verify\n    23\t     */\n    24\t    KeyMaterial load() throws CryptoException;\n    25\t\n    26\t    /**\n    27\t     * Human-readable identity for logs and error details. Nothing here is tunable at runtime on purpose.\n    10\t */\n    11\tpublic final class Limits {\n    12\t\n    13\t    /** Largest plaintext accepted by {@link SymmetricCrypto#encrypt}. */\n    14\t    public static final int MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES = 1 << 20; // 1 MiB\n    15\t\n    16\t    /** GCM nonce length. Fixed, never read from the record header beyond a consistency check. */\n    17\t    public static final int IV_BYTES = 12;\n    18\t\n    19\t    /** Ciphertext is plaintext plus the 16 byte GCM tag. */\n    20\t    public static final int MAX_CIPHERTEXT_BYTES = MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES + 16;\n    21\t\n    22\t    /** Largest encoded record string accepted by {@link EncryptedBlob#fromEncoded}. */\n    23\t    public static final int MAX_ENCODED_BLOB_CHARS = 4 * ((3 + IV_BYTES + MAX_CIPHERTEXT_BYTES) / 3) + 8;\n    24\t\n    25\t    /** GCM tag length in bits. Fixed at the maximum; shorter tags are rejected. */\n    26\t    public static final int TAG_BITS = 128;\n    27\t\n    28\t    /** Largest plaintext accepted through the String convenience overloads. */\n    29\t    public static final int MAX_PLAINTEXT_CHARS = MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES;\n    30\t\n    31\t    /** Allowed characters in a context string (which is authenticated as AES-GCM AAD). */\n    32\t    public static final int MAX_CONTEXT_CHARS = 200;\n    33\t\n    34\t    /** Lower bound for PBKDF2. Values below this are rejected rather than honoured. */\n    35\t    public static final int MIN_KDF_ITERATIONS = 310_000;\n    36\t\n    37\t    /** Upper bound for PBKDF2: above this a wrong-password attempt becomes a cheap DoS. */\n    38\t    public static final int MAX_KDF_ITERATIONS = 2_000_000;\n    39\t\n    40\t    private Limits() {\n    41\t    }\n    42\t\n    43\t    /**\n    44\t     * Validates a caller supplied byte array payload.\n    45\t     *\n    46\t     * @throws CryptoException {@code MISSING_INPUT} if null, {@code TOO_LARGE} if over {@code maxBytes}\n    47\t     */\n    48\t    public static byte[] requirePayload(byte[] value, int maxBytes, String what) throws CryptoException {\n    49\t        if (value == null) {\n    50\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.MISSING_INPUT, what + \" is missing\");\n    51\t        }\n    52\t        if (value.length > maxBytes) {\n    53\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.TOO_LARGE,\n    54\t                    what + \" exceeds the permitted size of \" + maxBytes + \" bytes\");\n    55\t        }\n    56\t        return value;\n    57\t    }\n    58\t\n    59\t    /**\n    60\t     * Validates a text field that must stay inside printable US-ASCII: no NULs, no newlines\n    61\t     * (log injection), no control characters, no bidi overrides.\n    62\t     *\n    63\t     * @throws CryptoException if the value is null, empty, too long or holds a disallowed code point\n    64\t     */\n    65\t    public static String requireAsciiText(String value, int maxChars, String what) throws CryptoException {\n    66\t        if (value == null || value.isEmpty()) {\n    67\t            throw new CryptoException(what + \" is missing\");\n    68\t        }\n    69\t        if (value.length() > maxChars) {\n    70\t            throw new CryptoException(what + \" exceeds the permitted length\");\n    71\t        }\n    72\t        for (int i = 0; An instruction to \"use a random\n    12\t * password\" with no way to make one safely produces hand-composed values, which is the exact failure\n    13\t * the length floor is trying to prevent.\n    14\t *\n    15\t * <p>Output is a {@code char[]} drawn from the printable ASCII range and contains no whitespace, so it\n    16\t * survives a line-oriented config file or an environment variable. It deliberately satisfies\n    17\t * {@link KeystoreKeySource#requirePassphrase}: no trailing {@code NUL}, no surrogates.\n    18\t *\n    19\t * <p><strong>It still needs quoting.</strong> The alphabet is every printable ASCII character except\n    20\t * space, which includes {@code $ ` \" ' \\ ! & ; | < > ( ) * ? # ~} - so the value is free of encoding\n    21\t * surprises but not of interpretation. Unquoted in a shell it is expanded, split and redirected;\n    22\t * unquoted in YAML it can parse as a flow sequence, a mapping or a comment. Handle it as a single-quoted\n    23\t * shell argument, or write it to a file created {@code 0600} and pass the path instead. This class does\n    24\t * not restrict the alphabet to dodge that, because a narrowed alphabet is what an attacker who could\n    25\t * influence generation would choose, and every character dropped costs a bit of entropy; see\n    26\t * {@link #ALPHABET_SIZE}.\n    27\t *\n    28\t * <p>Every character is drawn from a fresh {@link SecureRandom} draw, so there is no dependence on a\n    29\t * caller-supplied seed. Strength follows from the true alphabet size: a 48-character output carries\n    30\t * 48 * log2({@value #ALPHABET_SIZE}) which is about 315 bits. That is far past brute-force reach and\n    31\t * past the point of relevance - the store's defence against an <em>offline</em> attack is the PBKDF2\n    32\t * cost in {@link PasswordKeySource}, not this length - so generation is generous rather than minimal.\n    33\t *\n    34\t * <p>The result is a mutable {@code char[]} rather than a {@code String} precisely so the caller can\n    35\t * erase it. Handing back a {@code String} would leave the secret in the interned string heap,\n    36\t * un-erasable, for the life of the JVM. Copy it only where necessary and {@code Arrays.fill} the\n    37\t * original as soon as the store has been created.\n    38\t */\n    39\tpublic final class RandomPasswords {\n    40\t\n    41\t    /** Default length: well past the floor any of the key sources impose. */\n    42\t    public static final int DEFAULT_LENGTH = 48;\n    43\t\n    44\t    /** Rejected rather than silently clamped; 4 KiB is the maximum any key source accepts. */\n    45\t    public static final int MAX_LENGTH = 1024;\n    46\t\n    47\t    /**\n    48\t     * Printable ASCII, excluding space (which config parsers and shells mishandle). Every remaining\n    49\t     * printable character is included: {@code 0x21..0x7E}. Several of them are shell and YAML\n    50\t     * metacharacters, which is a quoting obligation on the caller, not a reason to drop them - see the\n    51\t     * class note.\n    52\t     */\n    53\t    private static final char[] ALPHABET =\n    54\t            \"!\\\"#$%&'()*+,-./0123456789:;<=>?@ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ[\\\\]^_`abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz{|}~\"\n    55\t                    .toCharArray();\n    56\t\n    57\t    /** Alphabet cardinality, so the strength claim is derived from the alphabet rather than asserted. */\n    58\t    public static final int ALPHABET_SIZE = ALPHABET.length;\n    59\t\n    60\t    private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n    61\t\n    62\t    private RandomPasswords() {\n    63\t    }\n    64\t\n    65\t    /** A {@link #DEFAULT_LENGTH}-character random secret. */\n    66\t    public static char[] generate() throws CryptoException {\n    67\t        return generate(DEFAULT_LENGTH);\n    68\t    }\n    69\t\n    70\t    /**\n    71\t     * @param length number of characters, {@code 1..}{@value #MAX_LENGTH}; a value outside the range is\n    72\t     *     rejected rather than clamped, so a typo cannot quietly produce a 1-character password\n    73\t     * @return a fresh {@code char[]} holding the secret. Erase it with {@code Arrays.fill} when done.\n    74\t     * @throws CryptoException if {@code length} is out of range or the platform has no random source\n    75\t     */\n    76\t    public static char[] generate(int length) throws CryptoException {\n    77\t        if (length < 1 || length > MAX_LENGTH) {\n    78\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT,\n    79\t                    \"length must be between 1 and \" + MAX_LENGTH);\n    80\t        }\n    81\t        char[] out = new char[length];\n    82\t        try {\n    83\t            for (int i = 0; i < length; i++) {\n    84\t                /*\n    85\t                 * Rejection sampling instead of `random.nextInt(ALPHABET.length)` would be equivalent\n    86\t                 * here - nextInt(bound) is already unbiased - but drawing a full int and reducing keeps\n    87\t                 * the alphabet a private detail and cannot leak index patterns.\n    88\t                 */\n    89\t                out[i] = ALPHABET[RANDOM.nextInt(ALPHABET.length)];\n    90\t            }\n    91\t            return out;\n    92\t        } catch (RuntimeException e) {\n    93\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(out, (char) 0);\n    94\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.PROVIDER_FAILURE,\n    95\t                    \"a random secret could not be generated: \" + e.getClass().getSimpleName(), e);\n    96\t        }\n    97\t    }\n    98\t}\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

A trailing newline is refused in\n    23\t *       the text form as well as the raw one: byte {@code 0x0A} is not printable, so it is rejected\n    24\t *       before the decoder is consulted. Trimming first is not an option - a value whose leading or\n    25\t *       trailing characters are removable is a value whose length somebody else decides, and the length\n    26\t *       is what distinguishes the raw form from the text form. The two forms are distinguishable by\n    27\t *       length alone, so there is no ambiguity to resolve and no \"try both and hope\" path;</li>\n    28\t *   <li>a buffer that is neither is rejected outright. There is no fallback that pads, truncates, or\n    29\t *       hashes the input down to 32 bytes - silently deriving a key from a malformed one is how a key\n    30\t *       rotation ends up with For the one legitimate case: an operator-facing\n    91\t     * export used to place a key into a secret manager.\n    92\t     *\n    93\t     * <p>The result is a {@code String} and therefore <em>not</em> erasable; it exists so the caller\n    94\t     * can hand a key to a system that only accepts text. Callers must not log it, must not put it in a\n    95\t     * command line, and must not write it to a file they have not created with {@code 0600}\n    96\t     * permissions. Prefer {@code PrivateFiles.writeOwnerOnlyFile} with raw bytes.\n    97\t     *\n    98\t     * <p>{@link KeyMaterial} is required rather than a bare array so the caller has already had to\n    99\t     * hold the key in a managed object.\n   100\t     */\n   101\t    public static String toBase64Url(KeyMaterial key) throws CryptoException {\n   102\t        byte[] raw = key.copyForWrite();\n   103\t        try {\n   104\t            return CANON.encodeToString(raw);\n   105\t        } finally {\n   106\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(raw, (byte) 0);\n   107\t        }\n   108\t    }\n   109\t}\n===== src/main/java/com/example/crypto/KeyFileKeySource.java =====\n     1\tpackage com.example.crypto;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.nio.file.Path;\n     4\t\n     5\t/**\n     6\t * Reads a key from a file on disk.\n     7\t *\n     8\t * <p>The file must be a regular file, not a symlink, owned by the user running this process, with mode\n     9\t * no looser than {@code 0600}, and every ancestor directory must be owned by that user and not\n    10\t * group- or world-writable (a world-writable directory is accepted only with the sticky bit, the\n    11\t * {@code /tmp} convention). Those checks are in {@link PrivateFiles} and they are performed before the\n    12\t * file is opened, not after.\n    13\t *\n    14\t * <p>Why not simply \"read the file and let the OS sort it out\": a key file is the one input an\n    15\t * unprivileged local attacker is most likely to be able to place, rename or symlink, because the path\n    16\t * normally comes from configuration. A path from configuration is still attacker-influenceable when\n    17\t * the config is world-writable, when it lives in a shared checkout, or when the deploy pipeline can be\n    18\t * nudged - so the path is validated, and the object behind it is inspected, rather than trusted.\n    19\t *\n    20\t * <p>A trailing newline is <strong>not</strong> accepted, in either accepted form. It would have to be\n    21\t * trimmed to be, and trimming is the thing not to do here: the length is what distinguishes a raw\n    22\t * 32-byte key from base64 text, so a value whose length is decided by whatever the last byte happens to\n    23\t * be is a value whose form is attacker- or editor-decided. A key file that will not load is a deploy\n    24\t * failure to fix at the deploy step - write the file with {@link KeyMaterial#writeTo}, which produces\n    25\t * exactly 32 raw bytes and nothing else - rather than something this class guesses about. See\n    26\t * {@link KeyEncoding}.\n    27\t */\n    28\tpublic final class KeyFileKeySource implements KeySource {\n    29\t\n    30\t    private final Path file;\n    31\t\n    32\t    /**\n    33\t     * @param file path to the key file. Relative paths are resolved against the process working\n    34\t     *     directory; prefer an absolute path from deployment configuration.\n    35\t     * @throws CryptoException if {@code file} is null, has no parent, or the path is longer than 4096\n    36\t     *     characters\n    37\t     */\n    38\t    public KeyFileKeySource(Path file) throws CryptoException {\n    39\t        if (file == null) {\n    40\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.MISSING_INPUT, \"key file path is missing\");\n    41\t        }\n    42\t        String abs = file.toAbsolutePath().toString();\n    43\t        if (abs.length() > 4096) {\n    44\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT, \"key file path is too long\");\n    45\t        }\n    46\t        if (abs.indexOf('\\0') >= 0) {\n    47\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT, \"key file path is malformed\");\n    48\t        }\n    49\t        this.file = file;\n    50\t    }\n    51\t\n    52\t    @Override\n    53\t    public KeyMaterial load() throws CryptoException {\n    54\t        /*\n    55\t         * The bound here is generous (KeyEncoding rejects anything over 4 KiB anyway) because the\n    56\t         * meaningful limit is on the decoded key, and a huge file must be refused before it is read.\n    57\t         */\n    58\t        byte[] raw = PrivateFiles.readOwnerOnlyFile(file, KeyEncoding.MAX_INPUT_BYTES);\n    59\t        try {\n    60\t            return KeyMaterial.fromBytes(KeyEncoding.decodeKeyBytes(raw));\n    61\t        } finally {\n    62\t            java.util.Arrays.fill(raw, (byte) 0);\n    63\t        }\n    64\t    }\n    65\t\n    66\t    /** The path. A path is not a secret; the file's contents are. */\n    67\t    @Override\n    68\t    public String describe() {\n    69\t        return \"file:\" + file.toAbsolutePath();\n    70\t    }\n    71\t\n    72\t    @Override\n    73\t    public boolean equals(Object o) {\n    74\t        return o instanceof KeyFileKeySource other && file.equals(other.file);\n    75\t    }\n    76\t\n    77\t    @Override\n    78\t    public int hashCode() {\n    79\t        return file.hashCode();\n    80\t    }\n    81\t\n    82\t    @Override\n    83\t    public String toString() {\n    84\t        return describe();\n    85\t    }\n    86\t}\n===== src/main/java/com/example/crypto/KeyMaterial.java =====\n     1\tpackage com.example.crypto;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.util.Arrays;\n     4\t\n     5\t/**\n     6\t * An AES key held as a private, copy-owned {@code byte[]} that this class wipes as soon as it is\n     7\t * finished with.\n     8\t *\n     9\t * <p>Why not hold a {@code SecretKey}? Because {@code SecretKeySpec} keeps a live copy of the key\n    10\t * for the lifetime of the object, and its {@code destroy()} does not erase anything - on this JDK it\n    11\t * throws {@code DestroyFailedException} and leaves {@code isDestroyed()} false - so the only way to\n    12\t * actually remove the bytes from the heap is to own the array and zero it. {@code KeyMaterial} owns\n    13\t * the array.\n    14\t *\n    15\t * <p>Instances are short-lived by construction: {@link #use(java.util.function.Function)} derives a\n    16\t * {@code SecretKey} from the bytes, hands it to the callback and zeroes its own working array in a\n    17\t * {@code finally} block, and {@link #destroy()} erases the master copy. Nothing else in this component\n    18\t * is handed the key, and {@code SymmetricCrypto} never retains a {@code KeyMaterial}.\n    19\t *\n    20\t * <p>What {@code use} does <em>not</em> erase is stated rather than glossed over: {@code SecretKeySpec}\n    21\t * clones the array it is given, so the {@code Cipher} inside the callback runs on the JDK's copy, which\n    22\t * no public API reaches. Zeroing {@code working} removes this class's copy only. That JDK copy becomes\n    23\t * unreachable when the callback returns and is collected on the ordinary schedule; erasing key material\n    24\t * held inside another library is not achievable from this class, and saying otherwise would make the\n    25\t * {@code finally} block look like a control stronger than it is. It is on the residual-risk list.\n    26\t *\n    27\t * <p>Security notes that are easy to get wrong and are enforced here:\n    28\t * <ul>\n    29\t *   <li>the constructor copies the caller's array, so wiping the caller's buffer cannot corrupt\n    30\t *       this object, and this object cannot corrupt the caller's buffer;</li>\n    31\t *   <li>{@link #equals}, {@link #hashCode} and {@link #toString} never expose the bytes;</li>\n    32\t *   <li>only 256-bit AES keys are accepted. {@code SecretKeySpec} would happily construct from a\n    33\t *       20-byte array and fail later, so the length is checked here, at the boundary.</li>\n    34\t * </ul>\n    35\t */\n    36\tpublic final class KeyMaterial implements AutoCloseable {\n    37\t\n    38\t    static final int KEY_BYTES = 32;\n    39\t\n    40\t    private final int version;\n    41\t    private byte[] key;\n    42\t    private boolean destroyed;\n    43\t\n    44\t    /**\n    45\t     * Imports key bytes supplied by the caller - from a key store, an environment variable or a\n    46\t     * secret manager. The bytes are copied, so the caller may (and should) zero its own buffer\n    47\t     * immediately; exactly {@value #KEY_BYTES} bytes are accepted and anything else is refused rather\n    48\t     * than padded, truncated or hashed down. The key is version 1.\n    49\t     */\n    50\t    public static KeyMaterial fromBytes(byte[] rawKey) throws CryptoException {\n    51\t        return new KeyMaterial(rawKey, EncryptedBlob.MIN_KEY_VERSION);\n    52\t    }\n    53\t\n    54\t    /**\n    55\t     * Imports key bytes tagged with a key version, for use with {@link KeyVault}. The version is not\n    56\t     * bookkeeping metadata: it is written into every record this key encrypts and included in the\n    57\t     * AES-GCM AAD, so a record is cryptographically bound to the key version that produced it and\n    58\t     * cannot be re-stamped to claim another one.\n    59\t     *\n    60\t     * @throws CryptoException if the version is outside {@code 1..255}\n    61\t     */\n    62\t    public static KeyMaterial fromBytes(byte[] rawKey, int keyVersion) throws CryptoException {\n    63\t        if (keyVersion < EncryptedBlob.MIN_KEY_VERSION || keyVersion > EncryptedBlob.MAX_KEY_VERSION) {\n    64\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT,\n    65\t                    \"key version must be between \" + EncryptedBlob.MIN_KEY_VERSION + \" and \"\n    66\t                            + EncryptedBlob.MAX_KEY_VERSION);\n    67\t        }\n    68\t        return new KeyMaterial(rawKey, keyVersion);\n    69\t    }\n    70\t\n    71\t    /** The key version this material is tagged with. */\n    72\t    public int version() {\n    73\t        return version;\n    74\t    }\n    75\t\n    76\t    KeyMaterial(byte[] rawKey, int version) throws CryptoException {\n    77\t        this.version = version;\n    78\t        if (rawKey == null) {\n    79\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY, \"key material is missing\");\n    80\t        }\n    81\t        if (rawKey.length != KEY_BYTES) {\n    82\t            /* Security: only AES-256. A shorter key is a downgrade, not a configuration option. */\n    83\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_KEY,\n    84\t                    \"expected a \" + KEY_BYTES + \"-byte key, got \" + rawKey.length);\n    85\t        }\n    86\t        this.key = rawKey.clone();\n    87\t    }\n    88\t\n    89\t    /**\n    90\t     * Runs {@code action} with a {@code SecretKey} derived from this key material, then erases both\n    91\t     * copies. The action's return value is passed through.\n    92\t     *\n    93\t     * @throws CryptoException if the key has already been destroyed\n    94\t     */\n    95\t    public synchronized <T> T use(java.util.function.Function<javax.crypto.SecretKey, T> action)\n    96\t            throws CryptoException {\n    97\t        if (destroyed || key == null) {\n    98\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_NOT_ACTIVE,\n    99\t                    \"key material has already been destroyed\");\n   100\t        }\n   101\t        byte[] working = key.clone();\n   102\t        try {\n   103\t            return action.apply(new javax.crypto.spec.SecretKeySpec(working, \"AES\"));\n   104\t        } finally {\n   105\t            Arrays.fill(working, (byte) 0);\n   106\t        }\n   107\t    }\n   108\t\n   109\t    /**\n   110\t     * Package-internal raw export, used by {@link #writeTo} and {@link KeyEncoding#toBase64Url}.\n   111\t     * Deliberately not public: there is no safe generic answer to \"give me the key bytes\", so the two\n   112\t     * supported destinations - a file created private, and a canonical base64url string the caller is\n   113\t     * told not to log - are the only exits, each with its own documented handling requirements.\n   114\t     */\n   115\t    synchronized byte[] copyForWrite() throws CryptoException {\n   116\t        if (destroyed || key == null) {\n   117\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_NOT_ACTIVE,\n   118\t                    \"key material has already been destroyed\");\n   119\t        }\n   120\t        return key.clone();\n   121\t    }\n   122\t\n   123\t    /**\n   124\t     * Writes the key to a new file that is private from its first byte, for a deployment that keeps\n   125\t     * keys as files. Re-stamping any of\n    23\t * them on a captured record makes decryption fail instead of downgrading anything - a record cannot be\n    24\t * retargeted at another key, re-labelled as context-free, or moved to another format version. The\n    25\t * parsers re-check the header anyway, so a malformed record is rejected as malformed rather than\n    26\t * reaching the cipher.\n    27\t *\n    28\t * <p>Encoded form, for a database column, a header or a queue message:\n    29\t *\n    30\t * <pre>\n    31\t *   {@code v1.<base64url-nopadding(record)>[.<context>]}\n    32\t * </pre>\n    33\t *\n    34\t * <ul>\n    35\t *   <li>the literal prefix is always {@code v1.}, so a caller can decide which key versions to load\n    36\t *       without trusting or fully parsing the attacker's payload - see {@link #peekKeyVersion};</li>\n    37\t *   <li>base64url without padding, decoded canonically: the bytes are re-encoded and compared, so a\n    38\t *       second spelling of the same bytes is refused. Otherwise two different strings could decrypt to\n    39\t *       the same record, and anything that deduplicated, cached or rate-limited on the string would\n    40\t *       treat them as distinct;</li>\n    41\t *   <li>the context, when present, is the final field and is length-delimited, so a context that\n    42\t *       itself contains {@code '.'} cannot smuggle an extra field. The stored text is exactly the input\n    43\t *       that {@link Limits#requireAsciiText} validated, so it stays readable; what is authenticated as\n    44\t *       AAD is an injective byte mapping of it (see {@code SymmetricCrypto#ascii}).</li>\n    45\t * </ul>\n    46\t *\n    47\t * <p>Instances are immutable and hold no plaintext. {@code context} is not key material and is not\n    48\t * secret - it travels in the encoded form - so never put in it a value you are trying to protect.\n    49\t */\n    50\tpublic final class EncryptedBlob {\n    51\t\n    52\t    /** The only record format version this build writes or accepts. */\n    53\t    public static final byte FORMAT_VERSION = 1;\n    54\t\n    55\t    /** Key versions are a single header byte. */\n    56\t    public static final int MIN_KEY_VERSION = 1;\n    57\t    public static final int MAX_KEY_VERSION = 255;\n    58\t\n    59\t    private static final String PREFIX = \"v1.\";\n    60\t    private static final char SEP = '.';\n    61\t    private static final Base64.Encoder ENCODER = Base64.getUrlEncoder().withoutPadding();\n    62\t    private static final Base64.Decoder DECODER = Base64.getUrlDecoder();\n    63\t\n    64\t    static final int HEADER_BYTES = 3 + Limits.IV_BYTES;\n    65\t    private static final int TAG_BYTES = Limits.TAG_BITS / 8;\n    66\t    private static final int MIN_PACKED_BYTES = HEADER_BYTES + TAG_BYTES;\n    67\t    static final int MAX_PACKED_BYTES = Limits.MAX_CIPHERTEXT_BYTES + HEADER_BYTES;\n    68\t\n    69\t    private final byte[] packed;\n    70\t    private final boolean hasContext;\n    71\t    private final String context;\n    72\t\n    73\t    private EncryptedBlob(byte[] packed, boolean hasContext, String context) {\n    74\t        this.packed = packed;\n    75\t        this.hasContext = hasContext;\n    76\t        this.context = context;\n    77\t    }\n    78\t\n    79\t    /** Internal factory used by the engine. 178\t             * The cipher would reject that regardless - the flag is AAD - but keeping the two\n   179\t             * representations of one fact from ever diverging beats reasoning about check encoding and key-version range. */\n   188\t    private static byte[] structural(byte[] raw) throws CryptoException {\n   189\t        if (raw == null) {\n   190\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.MISSING_INPUT, \"record is missing\");\n   191\t        }\n   192\t        /*\n   193\t         * Bound before copying. `clone` runs first here in the \"record format version \" + (copy[0] & 0xFF) + \" is not supported\");\n   205\t        }\n   206\t        if (copy[1] != 0 && copy[1] != 1) {\n   207\t            throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.INVALID_INPUT,\n   208\t                    \"record context flag is not a valid encoding\");\n   209\t        }\n   210\t        if ((copy[2] & 0xFF) valid\");\n   213\t        }\n   214\t        return copy;\n   215\t    }\n   216\t\n   217\t    /**\n   218\t     * Reads the key version from an encoded string without trusting anything else in it, so a caller\n   219\t     * can load exactly one candidate key instead of one per version. Returns {@code -1} when the\n   220\t     * supported prefix is absent or the payload is too short to contain the header.\n   221\t     *\n   222\t     * <p>Remaining risk, to be documented wherever this is used: the value is attacker-chosen. It may\n   223\t     * only ever narrow which key is <em>attempted</em>. It must not be treated as authorisation, and\n   224\t     * the record must still be parsed and authenticated normally - the key version is inside the AAD,\n   225\t     * so a record re-stamped to another version fails authentication whatever this returns. A caller\n   226\t     * that has no key for the returned version must fail closed rather than fall back to others;\n   227\t     * {@link KeyVault} does exactly that.\n   228\t     *\n   229\t     * <p>Cost is bounded against a hostile input: the string length is capped as in\n   230\t     * {@link #fromEncoded}, and only the four base64 characters that cover the header are decoded, so a\n   231\t     * multi-megabyte payload does not get base64-decoded in full just to read one byte that will be\n   232\t     * re-checked, and authenticated, later anyway.\n   233\t     */\n   234\t    public static int peekKeyVersion(String encoded) {\n   235\t        if (encoded == null || !encoded.startsWith(PREFIX)) {\n   236\t            return -1;\n   237\t        }\n   238\t        if (encoded.length() > Limits.MAX_ENCODED_BLOB_CHARS + Limits.MAX_CONTEXT_CHARS + 1) {\n   239\t            return -1;\n   240\t        }\n   241\t        String rest = encoded.substring(PREFIX.length());\n   242\t        int sep = rest.indexOf(SEP);\n   243\t        String b64 = sep < 0 ? rest : rest.substring(0, sep);\n   244\t        if (b64.length() < 4) {\n   245\t            return -1;\n   246\t        }\n   247\t        if (b64.length() > (MAX_PACKED_BYTES * 4) / 3 + 4) {\n   248\t            return -1;\n   249\t        }\n   250\t        /*\n   251\t         * The cheapest bound on \"is this record big enough to hold a header at all\" is the encoded\n   252\t         * length, and it costs nothing: the payload cannot be shorter than a header plus a tag.\n   253\t         */\n   254\t        if (b64.length() < MIN_ENCODED_RECORD_CHARS) {\n   255\t            return -1;\n   256\t        }\n   257\t        try {\n   258\t            /* 4 characters decode to exactly the 3 header bytes; the rest of the record is irrelevant here. */\n   259\t            byte[] header = DECODER.decode(b64.substring(0, 4));\n   260\t            if (header.length < 3 || header[0] != FORMAT_VERSION) {\n   261\t                return -1;\n   262\t            }\n   263\t            int v = header[2] & 0xFF;\n   264\t            return v < MIN_KEY_VERSION ? -1 : v;\n   265\t        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {\n   266\t            return -1;\n   267\t        }\n   268\t    }\n   269\t\n   270\t    /** Base64url-without-padding length of the shortest record the format allows. */\n   271\t    private static final int MIN_ENCODED_RECORD_CHARS = base64CharsFor(MIN_PACKED_BYTES);\n   272\t\n   273\t    private static int base64CharsFor(int bytes) {\n   274\t        int rem = bytes % 3;\n   275\t        return (bytes / 3) * 4 + (rem == 0 ? 0 : rem + 1);\n   276\t    }\n   277\t\n   278\t    /** The whole record, header and nonce included, as a defensive copy. Not secret; safe to store. */\n   279\t    public byte[] toBytes() {\n   280\t        return packed.clone();\n   281\t    }\n   282\t\n   283\t    /** Internal, non-copying view used by the cipher. The first two header fields are what the record\n   285\t     * claims and the last two are what the caller and key assert, so the tag enforces that the claim\n   286\t     * and the assertion agree. Here the cost is explicit, validated and carried in the\n    25\t * file: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 with {@link Limits#MIN_KDF_ITERATIONS} to\n    26\t * {@link Limits#MAX_KDF_ITERATIONS} iterations, about 0.4-2.0&nbsp;s per attempt on this hardware.\n    27\t *\n    28\t * <p>Why a wrapped key rather than \"derive the key from the passphrase and use it\": a derived key has\n    29\t * no way to prove it came from the right passphrase. Encrypt everything with a wrong-passphrase-derived\n    30\t * key and you get a file that decrypts to random bytes, and a later reader cannot tell corruption from\n    31\t * a failed guess. So the passphrase-derived key here is a <em>key-encryption key</em> whose only job is\n    32\t * to wrap a 256-bit random data key; the wrap is AES-256-GCM, and its tag is what proves the\n    33\t * passphrase. One crypto object, one failure mode, and the key that ever touches application data is\n    34\t * uniformly random rather than whatever the passphrase happened to hash to.\n    35\t *\n    36\t * <p>Container layout, big-endian, no padding:\n    37\t *\n    38\t * <pre>\n    39\t *   5  bytes  magic  {@code SPKB1}\n    40\t *   1  byte   KDF id, 1 = PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256\n    41\t *   4  bytes  iteration count, validated against {@link Limits} on every read\n    42\t *   1  byte   salt length, must be 16\n    43\t *   16 bytes  KDF salt, random per container (so two stores of one passphrase differ)\n    44\t *   12 bytes  wrap nonce\n    45\t *   4  bytes  wrapped-key length, must be 48 (32 key + 16 tag)\n    46\t *   48 bytes  AES-256-GCM(data key) under the derived KEK\n    47\t * </pre>\n    48\t *\n    49\t * <p>The KDF id, iteration count, salt and length are outside the AEAD tag, so they are supplied as\n    50\t * additional authenticated data: a container cannot be edited to lower its own iteration count, swap\n    51\t * its salt, or claim a different KDF without the unwrap failing. That is what makes the cost parameter\n    52\t * safe to read from a file at all - an unauthenticated cost field is a downgrade channel, since the\n    53\t * attacker's own file then chooses how much work their guess costs.\n    54\t *\n    55\t * <p>Every field is range-checked <em>before</em> the KDF runs, and a rejected parameter never causes\n    56\t * the derivation to happen: reading {@code iterations = 2000000000} from a hostile file and then\n    57\t * obediently grinding through it is a denial of service delivered by configuration.\n    58\t *\n    59\t * <p><strong>Not a rate limiter.</strong> The KDF makes each guess expensive; it does not cap the\n    60\t * number of guesses. If an attacker can submit passphrases through your application, throttle in the\n    61\t * caller, per source and per principal, and count failures - this component has no idea who is asking.\n    62\t *\n    63\t * <p>Passphrases are {@code char[]}, copied on construction, erased by {@link #destroy()}. Its copies become unreachable when this\n   282\t     *       method returns and are collected on the ordinary garbage-collector schedule, which is a\n   283\t     *       residual risk stated in the README, not a fix;</li>\n   284\t     *   <li>{@code getEncoded()} returns a clone, so the returned array is a copy the caller owns and\n   285\t     *       can actually zero. It refuses anything\n    32\t * shorter than {@value #MIN_PASSPHRASE_CHARS} characters, and\n    33\t * {@link RandomPasswords#generate()} exists so nobody has to invent one by hand.\n    34\t *\n    35\t * <p>For a human-memorable passphrase, do not use this class. The version a record was written under is inside\n    17\t * its header <em>and</em> inside its AES-GCM AAD, so it cannot be changed by whoever stores the\n    18\t * record - see {@link EncryptedBlob}.\n    19\t *\n    20\t * <p>Reads are strict: {@link #decrypt} and {@link #decryptEncoded} never try other keys. A record\n    21\t * naming version 3 is decrypted with version 3 or it fails, even if version 2 would happily open it.\n    22\t * Trying each key in turn until one works would turn a decrypt call into a key-confirmation oracle and\n    23\t * make the cost of a failed attempt grow with the number of keys, which is both an information leak and\n    24\t * a slow-down an attacker controls.\n    25\t *\n    26\t * <h2>Key material lifetime</h2>\n    27\t * This class holds <em>sources</em>, not keys. A key is loaded from its source, used inside one\n    28\t * {@code encrypt}/{@code decrypt} call, and destroyed before that call returns, so no key is resident\n    29\t * between operations. That is a real cost - a PBKDF2-backed source costs about a second per operation -\n    30\t * and it is paid deliberately: the alternative is a key that lives in the heap for the JVM's lifetime,\n    31\t * which is what {@link KeyMaterial} exists to avoid. If your source is expensive, wrap this class with\n    32\t * one that caches plaintext for a bounded time; do not \"optimise\" it by caching {@link KeyMaterial}\n    33\t * here.\n    34\t *\n    35\t * <h2>Concurrency</h2>\n    36\t * Sources are cached in a {@link ConcurrentHashMap}. Two threads asking for the same version\n    37\t * concurrently can each construct a source, and exactly one wins the cache slot; the loser is\n    38\t * {@linkplain #release released} after its call completes. Dropping that reference matters: sources own\n    39\t * erased-on-destroy passwords, so a reference left in a local variable by a losing thread is a password\n    40\t * that outlives the operation. The decision \"is this the source to use\" is made by the atomic\n    41\t * {@code putIfAbsent} itself - there is no check-then-act window.\n    42\t *\n    43\t * <h2>Rotation</h2>\n    44\t * {@link #rotate} is atomic under the vault's own lock: the new version must be strictly greater than\n    45\t * the highest version ever issued, so two concurrent rotations cannot both claim the same number and\n    46\t * silently orphan records written under it. Unbounded, forged records are a data-plane way to (a) fill the\n   371\t             * tracked-version table until rotate() refuses - disabling the emergency re-key control - and\n   372\t             * (b) pin a live passphrase-holding source per version forever.
