A record naming an impossible version is the same attack as a record whose\n   378\t             * ciphertext byte was flipped: both are forgeries, both are settled by the AES-GCM tag. An\n   379\t             * early return here would (i) cost less than a real tag check, making the ceiling on the\n   380\t             * version space measurable from response time, and (ii) put the vault's key estate into the\n   381\t             * log through the refusal's wording. Instead the caller gets a random key it will never\n   382\t             * recognise as random: the tag fails, and the answer is byte-for-byte the answer to a\n   383\t             * tampered record. Nothing is cached, no factory runs, no KDF runs.\n   384\t             */\n   385\t            if (keyVersion > highestIssued.get() || sources.size() >= MAX_VERSIONS) {\n   386\t                return decoySource(keyVersion);\n   387\t            }\n   388\t            KeySource created = factory.create(keyVersion);\n   389\t            if (created == null) {\n   390\t                throw new CryptoException(CryptoException.CryptoFailureCode.KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE,\n   391\t                        \"key source factory returned no source for version \" + keyVersion);\n   392\t            }\n   393\t            KeySource winner = sources.putIfAbsent(keyVersion, created);\n   394\t            if (winner != null) {\n   395\t                /*\n   396\t                 * Lost the race. The caller still gets a working source, and the loser is released by\n   397\t                 * the caller's finally block because it is not the cached one - see isCached.\n   398\t                 */\n   399\t                return created;\n   400\t            }\n   401\t            return created;\n   402\t        }\n   403\t    }\n   404\t\n   405\t    /**\n   406\t     * A source for a version this vault will never provision, yielding a fresh random key that\n   407\t     * authenticates nothing.\n   408\t     *\n   409\t     * <p>Two properties are load-bearing and easy to get wrong:\n   410\t     * <ul>\n   411\t     *   <li>the key is drawn from {@link SecureRandom} <em>per call</em>. A deterministic stand-in would\n   412\t     *       be a forgery channel: an attacker who knows the scheme can encrypt under it for an\n   413\t     *       out-of-range version, and this vault would then accept his record;</li>\n   414\t     *   <li>the material carries the version the record claimed, so the {@code key.version()} comparison\n   415\t     *       in the decrypt path stays behind the tag check instead of becoming a second, distinguishable\n   416\t     *       failure code.</li>\n   417\t     * </ul>\n   418\t     *\n   419\t     * <p>Nothing here is retained: the object is never stored in {@code sources}, it holds no secret\n   420\t     * between calls, and {@link #release(KeySource)} ignores it, so the caller's cleanup path is a\n   421\t     * no-op. Residual risk, stated rather than papered over: acquiring this key costs far less than\n   422\t     * acquiring a real one from a slow source, so \"no key is provisioned for that version\" remains\n   423\t     * observable through cost - as it already is through the difference between a cold and a warm cache.\n   424\t     */\n   425\t    private static KeySource decoySource(int keyVersion) {\n   426\t        return new DecoySource(keyVersion);\n   427\t    }\n   428\t\n   429\t    private static final java.security.SecureRandom DECOY_RANDOM = new java.security.SecureRandom();\n   430\t\n   431\t    private static final class DecoySource implements KeySource {\n   432\t\n   433\t        private final int keyVersion;\n   434\t\n   435\t        DecoySource(int keyVersion) {\n   436\t            this.keyVersion = keyVersion;\n   437\t        }\n   438\t\n   439\t        @Override\n   440\t        public KeyMaterial load() throws CryptoException {\n   441\t            byte[] filler = new byte[KeyMaterial.KEY_BYTES];\n   442\t            DECOY_RANDOM.nextBytes(filler);\n   443\t            try {\n   444\t                return KeyMaterial.fromBytes(filler, keyVersion);\n   445\t            } finally {\n   446\t                java.util.Arrays.fill(filler, (byte) 0);\n   447\t            }\n   448\t        }\n   449\t\n   450\t        @Override\n   451\t        public String describe() {\n   452\t            return \"vault:unprovisioned@\" + keyVersion;\n   453\t        }\n   454\t\n   455\t        @Override\n   456\t        public boolean equals(Object o) {\n   457\t            return o instanceof DecoySource other && keyVersion == other.keyVersion;\n   458\t        }\n   459\t\n   460\t        @Override\n   461\t        public int hashCode() {\n   462\t            return keyVersion * 31;\n   463\t        }\n   464\t\n   465\t        @Override\n   466\t        public String toString() {\n   467\t            return describe();\n   468\t        }\n   469\t    }\n     fastest = Math.min(fastest, us);\n   322\t            slowest = Math.max(slowest, us);\n   323\t        }\n   324\t        /* Microseconds, not nanoseconds: the resolution that matters for an oracle a client can observe. */\n   325\t        System.out.println(\"tamper-cost \" + report\n   326\t                + String.format(java.util.Locale.ROOT, \"| spread=%.2fx\", slowest / fastest));\n   327\t        assertTrue(slowest / fastest < 4.0, \"refusal cost spread too wide: \" + report);\n   328\t        /* A guard that refused before doing any work would also have a tight spread: require real work. */\n   329\t        assertTrue(fastest > 40, \"refusals implausibly cheap, so the AEAD is not attempted: \" + report);\n   330\t    }\n   331\t\n   332\t    /**\n   333\t     * The failure the tag check.\n   336\t     */\n   337\t    private static String authCodeOf(ThrowingRunnable r) {\n   338\t        try {\n   339\t            r.run();\n   340\t        } catch (CryptoException e) That is how a key ends up written into\n   360\t     * somebody else's file, or into a name the attacker controls.\n   361\t     *\n   362\t     * <p>The component closes the window by never asking the first question: the only decision is\n   363\t     * {@code CREATE_NEW}, made by the filesystem in the same operation that sets the mode. Since there is\n   364\t     * no window to lose, a *timed* race cannot observe the difference between the safe and the unsafe\n   365\t     * implementation - whichever process happens to arrive first wins the name either way. So the two\n   366\t     * orders are fixed here rather than raced, and the property that the window is gone is checked where\n   367\t     * it actually lives: in the source of the write path.\n   368\t     *\n   369\t     * <p>Both directions must hold, because the two failure modes are different: if the attacker holds the\n   370\t     * name, the key must not be written into his file; Use when deploying or configuring encryption for data at rest, establishing controls to meet compliance requirements, or reviewing an implementation during a security assessment.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: cryptography\ntags:\n- cryptography\n- encryption\n- aes\n- data-at-rest\n- symmetric-encryption\nversion: '1.0'\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.DS-01\n- PR.DS-02\n- PR.DS-10\nmitre_attack:\n- T1600\n- T1573\n- T1553\n- T1486\n---\n# Implementing AES Encryption for Data at Rest\n\n## Overview\n\nAES (Advanced Encryption Standard) is a symmetric block cipher standardized by NIST (FIPS 197) used to protect classified and sensitive data. This skill covers implementing AES-256 encryption in GCM mode for encrypting files and data stores at rest, including proper key derivation, IV/nonce management, and authenticated encryption.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When deploying or configuring implementing aes encryption for data at rest capabilities in your environment\n- When establishing security controls aligned to compliance requirements\n- When building or improving security architecture for this domain\n- When conducting security assessments that require this implementation\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Implement AES-256-GCM encryption and decryption for files\n- Derive encryption keys from passwords using PBKDF2 and Argon2\n- Manage initialization vectors (IVs) and nonces securely\n- Encrypt and decrypt entire directory trees\n- Implement authenticated encryption to detect tampering\n- Handle large files with streaming encryption\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### AES Modes of Operation\n\n| Mode | Authentication | Parallelizable | Use Case |\n|------|---------------|----------------|----------|\n| GCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | Yes            | Network data, file encryption |\n| CBC  | No            | Decrypt only   | Legacy systems, disk encryption |\n| CTR  | No            | Yes            | Streaming encryption |\n| CCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | No             | IoT, constrained environments |\n\n### Key Derivation\n\nNever use raw passwords as encryption keys. Always derive keys using:\n- **PBKDF2**: NIST-approved, widely supported (minimum 600,000 iterations as of 2024)\n- **Argon2id**: Winner of Password Hashing Competition, memory-hard\n- **scrypt**: Memory-hard, good alternative to Argon2\n\n### Nonce/IV Management\n\n- GCM requires a 96-bit (12-byte) nonce that must NEVER be reused with the same key\n- Generate nonces using `os.urandom()` (CSPRNG)\n- Store nonce alongside ciphertext (it is not secret)\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. Install the `cryptography` library: `pip install cryptography`\n2. Generate or derive an encryption key\n3. Create a random nonce for each encryption operation\n4. Encrypt data using AES-256-GCM with the key and nonce\n5. Store nonce + ciphertext + authentication tag together\n6. For decryption, extract nonce, verify tag, and decrypt\n\n## Encrypted File Format\n\n```\n[salt: 16 bytes][nonce: 12 bytes][ciphertext: variable][tag: 16 bytes]\n```\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Always use authenticated encryption (GCM, CCM) to prevent tampering\n- Never reuse a nonce with the same key (catastrophic in GCM)\n- Use at least 256-bit keys for long-term data protection\n- Securely wipe keys from memory after use when possible\n- Rotate encryption keys periodically per organizational policy\n- For disk-level encryption, consider XTS mode (AES-XTS)\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] AES-256-GCM encryption produces valid ciphertext\n- [ ] Decryption recovers original plaintext exactly\n- [ ] Authentication tag detects any ciphertext modification\n- [ ] Key derivation uses sufficient iterations/parameters\n- [ ] Nonces are never reused for the same key\n- [ ] Large files (>1GB) can be processed via streaming\n- [ ] Encrypted file format includes all necessary metadata\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

MEASURE-2.7\n- MAP-5.1\n- MANAGE-2.4\natlas_techniques:\n- AML.T0070\n- AML.T0066\n- AML.T0082\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- ID.RA-01\n- PR.DS-10\n- DE.CM-01\nmitre_attack:\n- T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1505.003\n- T1083\n---\n\n# Testing for Sensitive Data Exposure\n\n## When to Use\n\n- During authorized penetration tests when assessing data protection controls\n- When evaluating applications for GDPR, PCI DSS, HIPAA, or other data protection compliance\n- For identifying leaked API keys, credentials, tokens, and secrets in application responses\n- When testing whether sensitive data is properly encrypted in transit and at rest\n- During security assessments of APIs that handle PII, financial data, or health records\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- **Authorization**: Written penetration testing agreement with data handling scope\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: For intercepting and analyzing responses for sensitive data\n- **trufflehog**: Secret scanning tool (`pip install trufflehog`)\n- **gitleaks**: Git repository secret scanner (`go install github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks/v8@latest`)\n- **curl/httpie**: For manual endpoint testing\n- **Browser DevTools**: For examining local storage, session storage, and cached data\n- **testssl.sh**: TLS configuration testing tool\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Scan for Secrets in Client-Side Code\n\nSearch JavaScript files, HTML source, and other client-side resources for exposed secrets.\n\n```bash\n# Download and search JavaScript files for secrets\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/\" | \\\n  grep -oP 'src=\"[^\"]*\\.js[^\"]*\"' | \\\n  grep -oP '\"[^\"]*\"' | tr -d '\"' | while read js; do\n    echo \"=== Scanning: $js ===\"\n    # Handle relative URLs\n    if [[ \"$js\" == /* ]]; then\n      curl -s \"https://target.example.com$js\"\n    else\n      curl -s \"$js\"\n    fi | grep -inE \\\n      \"(api[_-]?key|apikey|api[_-]?secret|aws[_-]?access|aws[_-]?secret|private[_-]?key|password|secret|token|auth|credential|AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16})\" \\\n      | head -20\ndone\n\n# Search for common secret patterns\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/static/app.js\" | grep -nP \\\n  \"(AIza[0-9A-Za-z-_]{35}|AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}|sk-[a-zA-Z0-9]{48}|ghp_[a-zA-Z0-9]{36}|xox[bpsa]-[0-9a-zA-Z-]{10,})\"\n\n# Check source maps for exposed source code\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/static/app.js.map\" | head -c 500\n# Source maps may contain original source code with embedded secrets\n\n# Search HTML source for exposed data\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -inE \\\n  \"(api_key|secret|password|token|private_key|database_url|smtp_password)\" | head -20\n\n# Check for exposed .env or configuration files\nfor file in .env .env.local .env.production config.json settings.json \\\n  .aws/credentials .docker/config.json; do\n  status=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n    \"https://target.example.com/$file\")\n  if [ \"$status\" == \"200\" ]; then\n    echo \"FOUND: $file ($status)\"\n  fi\ndone\n```\n\n### Step 2: Analyze API Responses for Data Over-Exposure\n\nCheck if API endpoints return more data than necessary.\n\n```bash\n# Fetch user profile and examine response fields\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users/me\" | jq .\n\n# Look for sensitive fields that should not be exposed:\n# - password, password_hash, password_salt\n# - ssn, social_security_number, national_id\n# - credit_card_number, card_cvv, card_expiry\n# - api_key, secret_key, access_token, refresh_token\n# - internal_id, database_id\n# - ip_address, session_id\n# - date_of_birth, drivers_license\n\n# Check list endpoints for excessive data\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users\" | jq '.[0] | keys'\n\n# Compare public vs authenticated responses\necho \"=== Public ===\"\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/api/users/1\" | jq 'keys'\necho \"=== Authenticated ===\"\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users/1\" | jq 'keys'\n\n# Check error responses for information leakage\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"invalid\": \"data\"}' \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users\" | jq .\n# Look for: stack traces, database queries, internal paths, version info\n\n# Test for PII in search/autocomplete responses\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/search?q=john\" | jq .\n# May return full user records instead of just names\n```\n\n### Step 3: Test Data Transmission Security\n\nVerify that sensitive data is encrypted during transmission.\n\n```bash\n# Check TLS configuration\n# Using testssl.sh\n./testssl.sh \"https://target.example.com\"\n\n# Quick TLS checks with curl\ncurl -s -v \"https://target.example.com/\" 2>&1 | grep -E \"(SSL|TLS|cipher|subject)\"\n\n# Check for HTTP (non-HTTPS) endpoints\ncurl -s -I \"http://target.example.com/\" | head -5\n# Should redirect to HTTPS\n\n# Check for mixed content (HTTP resources on HTTPS pages)\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -oP \"http://[^\\\"'> ]+\" | head -20\n\n# Check if sensitive forms submit over HTTPS\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -oP 'action=\"[^\"]*\"'\n# Form action should use HTTPS\n\n# Check for sensitive data in URL parameters (query string)\n# URLs are logged in browser history, server logs, proxy logs, Referer headers\n# Look for: /login?username=admin&password=secret\n# /api/data?ssn=123-45-6789\n# /search?credit_card=4111111111111111\n\n# Check WebSocket encryption\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -oP \"(ws|wss)://[^\\\"'> ]+\"\n# ws:// is unencrypted; should only use wss://\n```\n\n### Step 4: Examine Browser Storage for Sensitive Data\n\nCheck local storage, session storage, cookies, and cached responses.\n\n```bash\n# Check what cookies are set and their security attributes\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -i \"set-cookie\"\n\n# In browser DevTools (Application tab):\n# 1. Local Storage: Check for stored tokens, PII, credentials\n# 2. Session Storage: Check for temporary sensitive data\n# 3. IndexedDB: Check for cached application data\n# 4. Cache Storage: Check for cached API responses containing PII\n# 5. Cookies: Check for sensitive data in cookie values\n\n# Common insecure storage patterns:\n# localStorage.setItem('access_token', 'eyJ...');  // XSS can steal\n# localStorage.setItem('user', JSON.stringify({email: '...', ssn: '...'}));\n# sessionStorage.setItem('credit_card', '4111...');\n\n# Check for autocomplete on sensitive forms\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/login\" | \\\n  grep -oP '<input[^>]*(password|credit|ssn|card)[^>]*>' | \\\n  grep -v 'autocomplete=\"off\"'\n# Password and credit card fields should have autocomplete=\"off\"\n\n# Check Cache-Control headers on sensitive pages\nfor page in /account/profile /api/users/me /transactions /billing; do\n  echo -n \"$page: \"\n  curl -s -I \"https://target.example.com$page\" \\\n    -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" | \\\n    grep -i \"cache-control\" | tr -d '\\r'\n  echo\ndone\n# Sensitive pages should have: Cache-Control: no-store\n```\n\n### Step 5: Scan Git Repositories and Source Code for Secrets\n\nSearch for accidentally committed secrets in version control.\n\n```bash\n# Check for exposed .git directory\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/.git/config\"\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/.git/HEAD\"\n\n# If .git is exposed, use git-dumper to download\n# pip install git-dumper\ngit-dumper https://target.example.com/.git /tmp/target-repo\n\n# Scan downloaded repository with trufflehog\ntrufflehog filesystem /tmp/target-repo\n\n# Scan with gitleaks\ngitleaks detect --source /tmp/target-repo -v\n\n# If GitHub/GitLab repository is available (authorized scope)\ntrufflehog github --org target-organization --token $GITHUB_TOKEN\ngitleaks detect --source https://github.com/org/repo -v\n\n# Common secrets found in repositories:\n# - AWS access keys (AKIA...)\n# - Database connection strings\n# - API keys (Google, Stripe, Twilio, SendGrid)\n# - Private SSH keys\n# - JWT signing secrets\n# - OAuth client secrets\n# - SMTP credentials\n\n# Search for secrets in Docker images\n# docker save target-image:latest | tar x -C /tmp/docker-layers\n# Search each layer for credentials\n```\n\n### Step 6: Test Data Masking and Redaction\n\nVerify that sensitive data is properly masked in the application.\n\n```bash\n# Check if credit card numbers are fully displayed\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/payment-methods\" | jq .\n# Should show: **** **** **** 4242, not full number\n\n# Check if SSN/national ID is masked\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users/me\" | jq '.ssn'\n# Should show: ***-**-6789, not full SSN\n\n# Check API responses for password hashes\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users\" | jq '.[].password // empty'\n# Should return nothing; password hashes should never be in API responses\n\n# Check export/download features for unmasked data\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users/export?format=csv\" | head -5\n# CSV exports often contain unmasked PII\n\n# Check logging endpoints for sensitive data\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/admin/logs\" | \\\n  grep -iE \"(password|token|secret|credit_card|ssn)\" | head -10\n# Logs should not contain sensitive data in plaintext\n\n# Test for sensitive data in error messages\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"email\":\"duplicate@test.com\"}' \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/register\"\n# Should not reveal: \"User with email duplicate@test.com already exists\"\n# Should show: \"Registration failed\" (generic)\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **Sensitive Data Exposure** | Unintended disclosure of PII, credentials, financial data, or health records |\n| **Data Over-Exposure** | API returning more data fields than the client needs |\n| **Secret Leakage** | API keys, tokens, or credentials exposed in client-side code or logs |\n| **Data at Rest** | Sensitive data stored in databases, files, or backups without encryption |\n| **Data in Transit** | Sensitive data transmitted over network without TLS encryption |\n| **Data Masking** | Replacing sensitive data with redacted values (e.g., showing last 4 digits of credit card) |\n| **PII** | Personally Identifiable Information - data that can identify an individual |\n| **Information Leakage** | Excessive error messages, stack traces, or debug information in responses |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Response analysis and regex-based sensitive data scanning |\n| **trufflehog** | Secret detection across git repos, filesystems, and cloud storage |\n| **gitleaks** | Git repository scanning for hardcoded secrets |\n| **testssl.sh** | TLS/SSL configuration assessment |\n| **git-dumper** | Downloading exposed .git directories from web servers |\n| **SecretFinder** | JavaScript file analysis for exposed API keys and tokens |\n| **Retire.js** | Detecting JavaScript libraries with known vulnerabilities |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: API Key in JavaScript Bundle\nThe application's JavaScript bundle contains a hardcoded Google Maps API key and a Stripe publishable key. The Stripe key has overly broad permissions, allowing the attacker to create charges.\n\n### Scenario 2: User API Returns Password Hashes\nThe `/api/users` endpoint returns complete user objects including bcrypt password hashes. Attackers can extract hashes and attempt offline cracking.\n\n### Scenario 3: PII in Cached API Responses\nThe user profile API endpoint returns full SSN and credit card numbers without masking. The endpoint does not set `Cache-Control: no-store`, so responses are cached in the browser and proxy caches.\n\n### Scenario 4: Git Repository with Database Credentials\nThe `.git` directory is accessible on the production server. Using git-dumper, the attacker downloads the repository history, finding database credentials committed in an early commit that were later \"removed\" but remain in git history.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Sensitive Data Exposure Assessment Report\n\n**Target**: target.example.com\n**Assessment Date**: 2024-01-15\n**OWASP Category**: A02:2021 - Cryptographic Failures\n\n### Findings Summary\n| Finding | Severity | Data Type |\n|---------|----------|-----------|\n| API keys in JavaScript source | High | Credentials |\n| Password hashes in API response | Critical | Authentication |\n| Unmasked SSN in user profile | Critical | PII |\n| Credit card number in export | High | Financial |\n| .git directory exposed | Critical | Source code + secrets |\n| Missing TLS on API endpoint | High | All data in transit |\n| Sensitive data in error messages | Medium | Technical info |\n\n### Critical: Exposed Secrets\n| Secret Type | Location | Risk |\n|-------------|----------|------|\n| AWS Access Key (AKIA...) | /static/app.js line 342 | AWS resource access |\n| Stripe Secret Key (sk_live_...) | .env (via .git exposure) | Payment processing |\n| Database URL with credentials | .git history commit abc123 | Database access |\n| JWT Signing Secret | config.json (via .git) | Token forgery |\n\n### Data Over-Exposure in APIs\n| Endpoint | Unnecessary Fields Returned |\n|----------|-----------------------------|\n| GET /api/users | password_hash, internal_id, created_ip |\n| GET /api/users/{id} | ssn, credit_card_full, date_of_birth |\n| GET /api/orders | customer_phone, customer_address |\n\n### Recommendation\n1. Remove all hardcoded secrets from client-side code; use backend proxies\n2. Rotate all exposed credentials immediately\n3. Remove .git directory from production web root\n4. Implement response field filtering; return only required fields\n5. Mask sensitive data (SSN, credit card) in all API responses\n6. Add Cache-Control: no-store to all sensitive endpoints\n7. Enable TLS 1.2+ on all endpoints; redirect HTTP to HTTPS\n8. Implement secret scanning in CI/CD pipeline (trufflehog/gitleaks)\n```\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

Nothing else — no CLI, no UI, no admin features.\n     6\t\n     7\tJDK 17, no runtime dependencies. The only dependency in the build is JUnit, test-scoped.\n     8\t\n     9\t```\n    10\tSymmetricCrypto crypto = new SymmetricCrypto();\n    11\tKeyMaterial key = crypto.generateKey(1);\n    12\t\n    13\tEncryptedBlob blob = crypto.encrypt(key, cardNumber, \"tenant:42\");   // context is authenticated\n    14\tString back = crypto.decryptToString(key, blob, \"tenant:42\");        // wrong context -> fails\n    15\t```\n    16\t\n    17\tIn a real deployment the key does not stay in memory forever — you register where it lives and let the\n    18\tvault load it per operation:\n    19\t\n    20\t```\n    21\tKeyVault vault = new KeyVault(v -> new KeyFileKeySource(Path.of(\"/etc/app/keys/v\" + v + \".key\")), 1);\n    22\tEncryptedBlob blob = vault.encryptString(cardNumber, \"tenant:42\");\n    23\tString back = vault.decryptStringEncoded(storedValue, \"tenant:42\");\n    24\t```\n    25\t\n    26\t## Build\n    27\t\n    28\t```\n    29\tmvn -o test      # 72 tests\n    30\tmvn -o package\n    31\t```\n    32\t\n    33\tRequires a JVM with the unlimited-strength crypto policy: the library refuses to load at all if\n    34\tAES-256 is unavailable, rather than failing on the first call.\n    35\t\n    36\t## What is in a record\n    37\t\n    38\t```\n    39\tbyte 0      format version (1)\n    40\tbyte 1      context flag   (0 or 1)\n    41\tbyte 2      key version    (1..255)\n    42\tbytes 3..14 nonce          (96 bits, SecureRandom, fresh per record)\n    43\tbytes 15..  ciphertext + 128-bit GCM tag\n    44\t```\n    45\t\n    46\tText form: `v1.<base64url-nopadding>[.<context>]`. The string form is the only one that carries the\n    47\tcontext, so `EncryptedBlob.fromBytes` deliberately refuses a context-bound record — a caller must not\n    48\tbe able to satisfy a context-bound record on the strength of an unauthenticated flag.\n    49\t\n    50\tThe AAD is `format || record's claimed context flag || record's claimed key version ||\n    51\tcaller-has-context || key's real version || encoded context`. Both readings of each header field are\n    52\tauthenticated, so a tampered header cannot be settled by a branch: there is **no early return** for a\n    53\tversion mismatch, and editing the version byte costs exactly as much as editing the ciphertext. An\n    54\timplementation that returned \"unknown key version\" before doing the AES-GCM work would leak, by timing\n    55\talone, which records name a version this server knows.\n    56\t\n    57\t## Which of the eight rules apply, and how\n    58\t\n    59\tRules 1, 2, 5, 6, 7 and 8 apply directly. Rules 3 and 4 mostly do not — see their sections; pretending\n    60\totherwise would be padding.\n    61\t\n    62\t### 1. Trust boundaries — every input is enumerated and validated before it can affect an outcome\n    63\t\n    64\t| Input | Where from | Validation before use |\n    65\t|---|---|---|\n    66\t| plaintext | caller / database / request body | non-null, ≤ 1 MiB (`Limits.MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES`), checked *before* any cipher or array work |\n    67\t| context string | caller, often derived from a request (`\"tenant:42\"`) | printable ASCII `0x21..0x7E` only, ≤ 200 chars — no NUL, no newline (log injection), no control chars, no bidi overrides; `null` means \"no context\" and is recorded as such rather than folded into `\"\"` |\n    68\t| encoded record | database, HTTP body, file, queue | length cap before scanning, no leading/trailing whitespace or control chars, prefix, field shape, strict base64url alphabet, **canonical re-encode equality**, then length/format/flag/version-range, then flag↔field agreement |\n    69\t| raw record bytes | same | size bounds, format-version whitelist (`UNSUPPORTED_FORMAT` for anything else), flag must be 0 or 1, key version ≥ 1 |\n    70\t| key text (`KeyEncoding.decodeKeyBytes`) | config file, env var, secret manager | exactly 32 raw bytes, or their canonical unpadded base64url text. Anything with whitespace, control characters, padding, a newline, or a non-canonical spelling is **refused, not repaired** |\n    71\t| environment variable name | config | `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]{0,63}`, checked **before** `System.getenv` is called |\n    72\t| file / directory path | config | non-null, ≤ 4096 chars, no NUL byte; then ownership, symlink, regular-file, mode and ancestor checks (rule 5) |\n    73\t| key version number | record header (`peekKeyVersion`), config, `addReadableKey` | `1..255` at the API, `1..64` tracked versions; a version never authorises anything (see below) |\n    74\t| PBKDF2 iteration count | the key file, i.e. an attacker-writable place | whitelist `310 000 .. 2 000 000`, checked **before** `SecretKeyFactory` is invoked |\n    75\t| passphrase | operator | 32..4096 chars; trailing NULs and unpaired surrogates refused, because `PBEKeySpec` is not injective over `char[]` and would silently map two passphrases to one |\n    76\t| keystore alias | config | `[A-Za-z0-9_.:\\-]{1,64}` |\n    77\t| `SecureRandom` | caller-supplied (optional constructor) | non-null; `getInstance` failures propagate as `PROVIDER_FAILURE`, never a silent fallback to `Random` |\n    78\t\n    79\tNothing in the API has a permissive default. There is no way to ask for AES-128, a shorter tag, a\n    80\tcaller-chosen nonce, or \"just decrypt it without the context\".\n    81\t\n    82\t`EncryptedBlob.peekKeyVersion(String)` is the one function that deliberately returns\n    83\t**attacker-chosen** bytes, so that a caller can load one candidate key instead of all of them. It is\n    84\tdocumented at the declaration as untrusted and advisory: it may only narrow which key is *attempted*.\n    85\tThe version byte is inside the AAD, so a record re-stamped to another version fails authentication no\n    86\tmatter what this returned, and `KeyVault` fails closed when it has no key for that version rather than\n    87\ttrying others. Residual risk to note where it is used: it is a cheap routing hint, so it is also a way\n    88\tfor an attacker to make a server attempt — and fail — a decryption. That is why the failure is\n    89\tconstant-cost (rule 6) and why there is no key-confirmation oracle.\n    90\t\n    91\t### 2. Secrets — no key or password is ever hardcoded, printed, logged, or passed in argv\n    92\t\n    93\t- No key material, salt, IV, passphrase or default password appears anywhere in the sources. A test\n    94\t  (`nothingThatLooksLikeAKeyIsEverDerivedFromAConstantOrADefault`) generates 64 keys and asserts they\n    95\t  are unique, non-zero and byte-varied — i.e. that the generator is really generating.\n    96\t- Keys come from `KeySource` implementations at runtime: a file, an environment variable, a PBKDF2\n    97\t  wrapped container, or a PKCS#12 keystore. Passwords are never stored in code or in the container —\n    98\t  `PasswordKeySource` holds the passphrase as a `char[]` and erases it.\n    99\t- `CryptoException.getMessage()` is one of exactly four fixed strings. The reason for a failure lives in\n   100\t  `getTechnicalDetail()`, which is a separate method a caller decides to log; no logger is invoked here,\n   101\t  and no detail contains key bytes, plaintext, a passphrase, or a file's contents.\n   102\t- `KeyMaterial.toString()` is `\"KeyMaterial[destroyed=…]\"`. `KeyVault.toString()` lists versions and\n   103\t  source descriptions (paths, env names) — locations, never secrets. `EncryptedBlob.toString()` prints\n   104\t  the record's own metadata, which is not secret.\n   105\t- `describe()` returns `env:NAME`, `file:/path`, `pbkdf2:/path@v3`, `pkcs12:/path#alias@v1`.\n   106\t- Nothing is passed through argv. The main sources declare no `main`, no `ProcessBuilder` and no\n   107\t  `Runtime.exec` at all — verified by grep, not by convention — so a secret can never appear in a\n   108\t  command line that another user can read from `ps`. (The test suite shells out once, to create a\n   109\t  world-readable file as a competing writer, and its helper child JVM takes only a variable *name* and\n   110\t  prints a hash of the key, never the key.)\n   111\t- A sweeping test (`everyFailurePathRendersNothingAnAttackerWants`) walks 20 failure routes and renders\n   112\t  `getMessage`, `getLocalizedMessage`, `getTechnicalDetail`, `toString`, `code`, `getCause`,\n   113\t  `getSuppressed` and `printStackTrace` for each, then asserts that no key hex, no key base64, no\n   114\t  plaintext and no passphrase appears anywhere in the result.\n   115\t\n   116\t### 3. Authentication and access control — largely inapplicable, and what replaces it\n   117\t\n   118\tThere is no authentication, session, account or privilege model in a crypto library: it has no users to\n   119\tauthenticate and no access-control decision to make. Two things *are* in scope, and both fail closed:\n   120\t\n   121\t- **A key version is never an authorisation decision.** `decrypt` attempts exactly the one key a record\n   122\t  names and never tries others, so there is no \"did some key open this?\" oracle to probe. If the source\n   123\t  for a version hands back a key that mislabels itself, that is refused as `INVALID_KEY` — a\n   124\t  configuration fault, reported as one, on both the encrypt and the decrypt side.\n   125\t- **Fail closed, always.** No key source returns a default, a zeroed key or a cached last-good key. A\n   126\t  missing env var, an unreadable file, a wrong password, a disabled version, a closed vault, a source\n   127\t  that returns `null`, a filesystem without POSIX permissions — every one is an exception. `close()` is\n   128\t  sticky: a closed vault refuses forever rather than silently re-running the factory and re-deriving the\n   129\t  key it just erased (a real bug found by the tests, since fixed and now pinned by\n   130\t  `closingTheVaultMakesItFailClosedRatherThanRecreateAKey`).\n   131\t\n   132\t**Rate limiting is explicitly not implemented and cannot be** — the component has no request context to\n   133\tlimit per. Whoever calls this must rate-limit per source *and* per account before calling, especially\n   134\tfor `PasswordKeySource.load()`, where each attempt is one offline-guess trial. Measured on this\n   135\tmachine, the KDF floor of 310 000 iterations costs ~462 ms of CPU per attempt and the 2 000 000 ceiling\n   136\t~2.92 s — which is why the ceiling is enforced *before* the KDF runs. That is the cost of one trial, not a\n   137\tsubstitute for limiting trials.\n   138\t\n   139\t### 4. Identifiers and tokens — mostly inapplicable; the parts that apply are generated here\n   140\t\n   141\tThere are no session cookies, CSRF tokens or public identifiers, so cookie flags (`HttpOnly`,\n   142\t`SameSite`, `Secure`) have nothing to attach to. What applies:\n   143\t\n   144\t- **Every cryptographic value this component produces comes from its own `SecureRandom`**: 256-bit data\n   145\t  keys, 96-bit per-record nonces, 128-bit wrapping nonces, 128-bit PBKDF2 salts, wrapped-key containers,\n   146\t  and `RandomPasswords` output (48 chars by default from the 94 printable ASCII characters excluding\n   147\t  space, ≤ 1024, via the unbiased `nextInt(bound)`). Nothing is derived from a timestamp, a counter,\n   148\t  `UUID`, `Random`, or a caller-supplied seed.\n   149\t- **No caller can supply a nonce, an IV, a salt or a key-version generator.** The API takes none of them.\n   150\t- Key versions are assigned by `KeyVault.rotate()` as strictly increasing integers and are never reused,\n   151\t  even after a version is dropped.\n   152\t- External identifiers that *are* accepted are validated strictly (rule 1's table): key version numbers,\n   153\t  env var names, aliases, paths. Residual risk for `peekKeyVersion` is documented above.\n   154\t- `RandomPasswords` exists so that deployers stop inventing passwords; it is the only place in the code\n   155\t  that mints a human-usable secret.\n   156\t\n   157\t### 5. Sensitive data — masked in logs, written only to owner-private files, size-bounded, erased from memory\n   158\t\n   159\t- **Bound before allocating.** Size caps are checked before any allocation, base64 scan, KDF or cipher\n   160\t  call, so a 4 GB blob string cannot turn into a 4 GB array, and `iterations = 2000000000` in a hostile\n   161\t  key file is refused in microseconds (the test asserts the refusal costs under 60 ms).\n   162\t- **Files.** `PrivateFiles.writeOwnerOnlyFile` creates with `FileChannel.open(CREATE_NEW, …)` *and*\n   163\t  `unix:mode 0600` as a creation attribute, so the file is never born readable and there is no\n   164\t  create-then-chmod window. It never overwrites. `newOwnerOnlyOutputStream` exists for the one caller\n   165\t  that must write the bytes itself (`KeyStore.store`) — `Files.newOutputStream` has no `FileAttribute`\n   166\t  overload, so a keystore written that way is born `0644`; that is a real window, so the wrapper is used\n   167\t  instead and the mode is asserted afterwards.\n   168\t- **Reads** re-verify on *every* load, not once: not a symlink, a regular file, mode ⊆ `0600` (group,\n   169\t  other and every execute bit refused), no set-id bits, owned by this user, and **every ancestor\n   170\t  directory** checked — refusing anything writable by others without the sticky bit (`/tmp` is `1777`, so\n   171\t  the sticky convention is honoured while a genuinely unsafe `0775` is refused). After a read through a\n   172\t  shared parent, the file is re-identified rather than re-trusted: the parent's mode, the file's\n   173\t  `st_dev`/`st_ino` identity, its mode and its owner are all compared with what was snapshotted before the\n   174\t  read, and any disagreement discards the bytes. Checking only the parent's mode — which is what this used\n   175\t  to do — cannot detect the attack it documented: an attacker who can move entries in a shared parent can\n   176\t  swap the file for another inode while the mode and the parent's mode both stay put.\n   177\t- **Memory.** `KeyMaterial` owns its `byte[]` and zeroes it in a `finally` after every use, plus\n   178\t  `destroy()`/`close()`. The working copies, nonces and plaintext buffers inside the engine are zeroed\n   179\t  too. `SecretKeySpec.destroy()` is *not* relied on — on this JDK it throws `DestroyFailedException`,\n   180\t  leaves `isDestroyed()` false and zeroes neither its password nor its key; the only erasure that works is\n   181\t  the one this code performs on the array it owns. `SecretKeySpec` **clones** its input, so erasing the\n   182\t  caller's array does not touch the spec's copy and vice versa — `KeyMaterial.use()`'s `finally` therefore\n   183\t  erases only this class's own copy, which is what its javadoc claims and nothing more.\n   184\t  `PasswordKeySource` and `KeystoreKeySource` are `AutoCloseable` so a try-with-resources erases the\n   185\t  passphrase. A caller's arrays are never mutated: `KeyMaterial.fromBytes` clones, and the record's\n   186\t  `toBytes()` returns a copy (`ciphertext()` is package-private precisely so the non-copying view cannot\n   187\t  escape).\n   188\t- **What actually erases a derived passphrase.** `PBEKeySpec.clearPassword()` does erase: measured on this\n   189\t  JDK (JDK 17.0.20.1) it nulls the spec's internal `char[]`, and it leaves the *caller's* array untouched,\n   190\t  which is why the caller's array is wiped separately. `derive()` keeps the spec in a local and calls\n   191\t  `clearPassword()` in a `finally`, so the derived-key window does not depend on the caller closing\n   192\t  anything. The remaining hole is inside the provider: `PBKDF2KeyImpl` keeps its own `passwd` and `key`\n   193\t  arrays, and its `destroy()` throws rather than clearing them, so a derived key's copy in JDK internals\n   194\t  is beyond reach. Probed, measured, documented here rather than promised in a javadoc.\n   195\t- **Logs.** Nothing is logged. The only channel is `getTechnicalDetail()`, which names the failed\n   196\t  *requirement* (e.g. `key file permissions grant more than owner read/write: rw-rw-r--`) — a mode and a\n   197\t  path, which is what an operator needs, never contents.\n   198\t\n   199\t### 6. Failure behaviour — identical messages, comparable work, no existence oracle\n   200\t\n   201\tFour user-visible strings, no more:\n   202\t\n   203\t| code(s) | `getMessage()` |\n   204\t|---|---|\n   205\t| `MISSING_INPUT` | `No data was supplied` |\n   206\t| `INVALID_INPUT`, `UNSUPPORTED_FORMAT`, `TOO_LARGE` | `The supplied data is not in a supported format` |\n   207\t| `KEY_UNAVAILABLE`, `KEY_NOT_ACTIVE`, `AUTHENTICATION_FAILED`, `INVALID_KEY`, `KEY_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE`, `INSECURE_KEY_STORAGE` | `The data could not be decrypted` |\n   208\t| `PROVIDER_FAILURE` | `The data could not be processed` |\n   209\t\n   210\t- A forged record, a record for a version this vault does not track, a record whose key is disabled, a\n   211\t  record decrypted with the wrong key, and a record whose context does not match all produce the **same\n   212\t  sentence** — so nothing confirms that a record, an account, or a tenant exists. A test asserts the\n   213\t  refusal text names none of: key, password, secret, tenant, context, version, permission, file,\n   214\t  environment, keystore, tamper.\n   215\t- **The version table is never a leak, and an unknown version is answered with the tag.** A record naming\n   216\t  a version above every version this vault ever issued used to be refused by name — *\"no key source for\n   217\t  version 200\"* — which is a distinct, cheaper message on a path where the attacker controls the input, and\n   218\t  it also wrote the size of the key estate into the log. `sourceFor` now refuses nothing for such a record:\n   219\t  it hands back a stand-in source whose 32 bytes come from a fresh `SecureRandom` draw per call, so the\n   220\t  caller pays one full AES-GCM attempt and sees an ordinary `AUTHENTICATION_FAILED`, and the factory is\n   221\t  never invoked (a test asserts both the identical detail and that no factory ran). The stand-in must not\n   222\t  be a deterministic value — a fixed stand-in for a version the vault will never provision would be a\n   223\t  forgery channel, since anyone could encrypt under it — and it claims the *record's* version, so the\n   224\t  `key.version()` cross-check stays behind the tag rather than becoming the oracle that was removed. The\n   225\t  same ceiling is enforced on both insertion paths, so neither `addReadableKey` nor `rotate` can grow the\n   226\t  version table past `MAX_VERSIONS` and make the bound a lie.\n   227\t- **An early return in a guard is itself the oracle.** The lesson above generalised: a check that refuses\n   228\t  before the cipher runs differs from a tag failure in cost even when the text is copied, so every guard on\n   229\t  the decrypt path either runs before any attacker-controlled record is parsed (size caps, which refuse\n   230\t  cheaply and *must* refuse cheaply) or is folded into the AAD and paid for. The two are distinguished in\n   231\t  tests on purpose: caps assert the refusal is under a time bound, tamper asserts the refusal is *not*\n   232\t  cheap.\n   233\t- **Comparable work** is the harder half and it is enforced, not assumed: the version/context mismatch is\n   234\t  not returned early but folded into the AAD, so every refusal pays for one full AES-GCM attempt.\n   235\t  `editingTheHeaderCostsTheSameAsEditingTheCiphertext` times seven routes (edited version byte, context\n   236\t  flag, nonce byte, first body byte, last tag byte, wrong context, wrong key) and asserts the slowest/fastest\n   237\t  ratio stays under 4 and the fastest is above 40 µs. A cheaper guard was tried and removed for exactly\n   238\t  this reason: two layers refusing the same invariant make the inner one invisible to a boolean test, so\n   239\t  the *cost* is what proves it ran.\n   240\t- Stack traces and suppression are disabled (`super(message, cause, false, false)`), `printStackTrace`\n   241\t  emits only the class name and the generic message, and `getCause()` is sanitised to a\n   242\t  class-name-only wrapper, so `javax.crypto.*` internals and file paths cannot ride out on a cause chain.\n   243\t- **Serialization keeps the safe half.** `technicalDetail` is `transient` and the `code` is not, so an\n   244\t  exception that crosses a JVM — a queue, a session replica, a cache — keeps its generic message and its\n   245\t  machine-readable kind and loses the operator-facing detail. That asymmetry is deliberate and pinned by a\n   246\t  test that round-trips a real failure: message and code survive, `getTechnicalDetail()` comes back `null`.\n   247\t  (`code` was `transient` until a reviewer pointed out that a deserialised exception then reported *no* kind\n   248\t  at all, which pushes callers to log the message — the one thing that must stay generic — or invent a\n   249\t  classification from the text.)\n   250\t- Details go to the log channel only, and there they *do* differ — deliberately, or the operator's log is\n   251\t  useless. The test asserts the three authentication routes share one detail (they are one tag check)\n   252\t  while auth vs format vs missing differ from each other.\n   253\t- Unknown input is refused, never defaulted: unknown format version → `UNSUPPORTED_FORMAT`; non-canonical\n   254\t  base64 → refused; padded base64 → refused; a 33-byte key file (a raw key with a newline) → refused\n   255\t  rather than trimmed; a context bound record with a missing context field → refused.\n   256\t\n   257\t### 7. Concurrency — check-then-act made atomic\n   258\t\n   259\t- `KeyVault` keeps `ConcurrentHashMap` of *sources* (never keys) and guards every version transition\n   260\t  (`rotate`, `addReadableKey`, `disable`, `enable`, `sourceFor`'s cache fill) under one `rotationLock`, so\n   261\t  \"is this version still active?\" and \"use it\" cannot interleave with a rotation. `disable` cannot take\n   262\t  out the active version; `addReadableKey` cannot claim the active or a future version; `disable` refuses a\n   263\t  number outside `1..255` instead of recording it as a disabled version that can never exist, and neither\n   264\t  insertion path can push the table past `MAX_VERSIONS`.\n   265\t- Replacing a slot releases the object it displaced **only if no other version still points at it**. The\n   266\t  obvious code released the displaced source unconditionally, which erased a key that another version was\n   267\t  still serving — a `KeyVault.of(...)` that installs one source under two versions, or a caller that hands\n   268\t  the same `KeySource` to two `addReadableKey` calls, would lose live key material with no error. The test\n   269\t  wires a shared PKCS#12 source under two versions, rotates, replaces, and asserts the source is neither\n   270\t  destroyed nor unreadable afterwards.\n   271\t- A cache-miss fill uses `putIfAbsent`, and the loser of that race is released by the caller's `finally`\n   272\t  (tracked by `isCached`) — so a losing `KeyMaterial` is erased rather than leaked, and no source is\n   273\t  double-released.\n   274\t- **File creation is the atomic step.** The original code asked `Files.exists(file)` and then created the\n   275\t  file — two decisions about one fact, with a window in between for a local attacker to create that name\n   276\t  and have a key written into their file. The pre-check is now gone: `CREATE_NEW` is the decision, made\n   277\t  inside the one operation that also sets the `0600` mode.\n   278\t- **How that claim is tested, and why it is not tested with a race.** An earlier version of this test\n   279\t  raced a second process against the writer and asserted the race had actually been won. It was\n   280\t  unfalsifiable: because `CREATE_NEW` legitimately decides the contest whichever way the timing falls, the\n   281\t  racer never once observed a difference across 120 attempts, and the test could not have failed for any\n   282\t  implementation. Two tests replace it, and both can fail.\n   283\t  `aKeyFileClaimedBySomebodyElseIsNeverWrittenInto` fixes the two orders instead of gambling on one: the\n   284\t  attacker holds the name (`sh -c 'umask 000; set -C; : > file'`, so his `0666` file exists first) and our\n   285\t  key must not reach it — the write is refused, his file keeps its size and mode, and a byte search proves\n   286\t  the key is absent; then we hold the name and the key must never exist in a file whose mode somebody else\n   287\t  set (`0600`, our bytes). `theWritePathAsksTheFilesystemRatherThanCheckingFirst` checks the property where\n   288\t  it actually lives — in the source. It reads the two write methods with comments stripped (the code\n   289\t  deliberately *prose*s about not checking for existence, so a naive scan finds the banned token in the\n   290\t  explanation and reports the defect it is looking for) and requires `CREATE_NEW` plus `FileChannel.open`\n   291\t  while forbidding any existence query **about the target**. The probes are qualified with the target's\n   292\t  variable name because asking whether the parent *directory* exists is a different question, with no\n   293\t  competing writer, and refusing it would be an outage rather than a defence. A redundant `Files.exists`\n   294\t  in front of `CREATE_NEW` is invisible in behaviour — the filesystem still refuses — so a source guard is\n   295\t  the only thing that can see it, and the mutation harness proves the guard sees it: the\n   296\t  `target-existence-pre-check` mutant re-adds exactly that pre-check, changes no observable behaviour, and\n   297\t  is killed only by reading the code. For that to be honest the guard has to read the *mutant*, so the\n   298\t  lookup honours `-Dsecure.data.crypto.src.main=<dir>`; without the override it resolves from the compiled\n   299\t  classes, not from the working directory, which is why it survives the harness running elsewhere.\n   300\t- `KeyMaterial.use` is `synchronized` and single-purpose; `destroy()` is idempotent; `SymmetricCrypto`\n   301\t  is immutable apart from the thread-safe `SecureRandom`. Concurrency tests round-trip 8×25 (vault) and\n   302\t  8×40 (engine) operations, and the vault's test additionally asserts one source per version and that a\n   303\t  record superseded by a rotation fails as `KEY_UNAVAILABLE` — never as `AUTHENTICATION_FAILED`, which\n   304\t  would mean it was opened with the wrong key.\n   305\t\n   306\t### 8. Prefer the platform\n   307\t\n   308\tEverything cryptographic is the JDK's own, pinned in `pom.xml` as `<release>17</release>`:\n   309\t\n   310\t| need | what is used | why not a library |\n   311\t|---|---|---|\n   312\t| cipher | `javax.crypto.Cipher` / `AES/GCM/NoPadding` | a hand-written AEAD is the thing to avoid, not the deliverable |\n   313\t| randomness | `java.security.SecureRandom` | covers the OS entropy source; no JNR/`/dev/urandom` shim |\n   314\t| password-based key derivation | `SecretKeyFactory` / `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256` | standard, auditable, no Bouncy Castle needed |\n   315\t| key storage | `java.security.KeyStore` (PKCS#12) | the platform's own container format |\n   316\t| encodings | `java.util.Base64` URL encoder/decoder, `HexFormat` | — |\n   317\t| file permissions | `Files.getAttribute(\"unix:mode\")`, `PosixFilePermissions`, `FileChannel.open` with creation attributes | no JNI, no `chmod` shell-out in production code |\n   318\t| charset | `StandardCharsets.UTF_8` with `CodingErrorAction.REPORT` | malformed UTF-8 is refused, not silently replaced with `?` |\n   319\t\n   320\t**Third-party dependencies: none at runtime.** The shipped jar imports nothing outside `java.*` and\n   321\t`javax.crypto.*`. The single declared dependency is `org.junit.jupiter:junit-jupiter:5.10.2`,\n   322\t`<scope>test</scope>`, from Maven Central; this build runs with `mvn -o` against jars already in the\n   323\tlocal repository, and the version is pinned in the POM rather than resolved by a range. Because no\n   324\tthird-party code is fetched or executed, \"verify the download\" and \"never run code from a\n   325\tworld-writable location\" reduce to: nothing here is loaded from a path the component controls, classes\n   326\tload from the deployed artifact only, and the *data* paths it does touch are subjected to the rule-5\n   327\tchecks (owner, mode, symlink, ancestor) that make a world-writable location a refusal rather than a\n   328\tsilent risk.\n   329\t\n   330\t## Residual risks — what this does *not* protect you from\n   331\t\n   332\t1. **It runs as root on this machine, and root reads everything.** A `0000` key file is refused because\n   333\t   the *mode* is wrong, but if the mode were merely odd root could still read it. These guards protect\n   334\t   against unprivileged users, other service accounts and configuration mistakes. They cannot protect a\n   335\t   key from a process that already owns the machine. (The suite prints this as a `NOTE` rather than\n   336\t   asserting it, and the ownership check cannot be exercised negatively while `user.name` is root.)\n   337\t2. **POSIX permissions are required.** On a filesystem without them (NTFS, FAT, some NFS/SMB mounts,\n   338\t   containers with odd mounts) `unix:mode` is unsupported and the component refuses to proceed — that is\n   339\t   a refusal, not a downgrade. There is no ACL or SELinux support; deploy a KMS if the key must never be\n   340\t   readable by the app's own user.\n   341\t3. **Random 96-bit nonces are unique with probability, not by construction.** After roughly 2³² records\n   342\t   under one key a nonce collision becomes likely, which for GCM is catastrophic. The component cannot\n   343\t   see how many records a key has produced across processes, so `KeyVault.rotate()` is the control and\n   344\t   must be used routinely. If you will write more than ~10⁸ records under one key, rotate before you\n   345\t   get there.\n   346\t4. **`peekKeyVersion` reads attacker-chosen bytes.** Documented at the declaration; usable only as a\n   347\t   routing hint; the tag is the authority. A caller that treats it as authorisation reintroduces the\n   348\t   retargeting attack this format was designed to defeat. Because it is the one API that accepts a\n   349\t   record-shaped string *without* authenticating anything, it is also the natural place to aim a\n   350\t   resource-exhaustion or scan-cost attack, so its own work is bounded and measured: the length caps are\n   351\t   applied to the string before any decode, the decode touches only the first 4 characters (3 header\n   352\t   bytes) whatever the input size, a record too short to hold a header is refused on encoded length alone,\n   353\t   and every failure returns the same `-1` with no exception. The test feeds it 8 MiB and 64 MiB-shaped\n   354\t   junk and asserts the refusal costs nothing (`peek-oversized 0us` in the build log) as well as asserting\n   355\t   the correct version for real records, so the bound is not achieved by always answering `-1`.\n   356\t5. **PKCS#12's internal KDF is cheap.** A keystore protected by a human-chosen passphrase is a target.\n   357\t   `MIN_PASSPHRASE_CHARS = 32` is a floor, not a recommendation: use `RandomPasswords`, or a random\n   358\t   store password from a secret manager. The container is a *container*, not a lock.\n   359\t6. **`PasswordKeySource` protects an offline guess with 310 000 PBKDF2 iterations** (~462 ms of CPU per\n   360\t   attempt here). That is one guess's cost, not a rate limit. Attempt limiting and lockout belong to the\n   361\t   caller — and a leaked container file is a *file*, so an attacker can run the guesses in parallel on\n   362\t   their own hardware at whatever cost they can afford.\n   363\t7. **No key use limits, no revocation, no crypto-shredding.** Rotation stops *new* reads through an old\n   364\t   version by default, but old ciphertexts remain decryptable if you `enable` the old version — the\n   365\t   caller decides, explicitly. There is no per-key record counter and no audit log. `close()` is likewise\n   366\t   not a barrier: a `decrypt` already past its key load when `close()` runs is not waited for. It cannot\n   367\t   succeed with erased material — the derivation/unwrap or the tag then fails, so it errors out — but do\n   368\t   not treat `close()` as \"no operation is in flight\". Quiesce traffic first if you need that guarantee.\n   369\t8. **Erasing memory is best-effort.** Zeroing the arrays this code owns is real; the JVM may still have\n   370\t   copied key bytes into a `SecretKeySpec` it owns, into a page file, or into a heap dump it was asked\n   371\t   to write. Swap, hibernation and core dumps are outside this component. Do not run this where an\n   372\t   attacker can read the process's memory and call the result safe.\n   373\t9. **Timing parity is a measured ratio, not a constant.** Seven tamper routes (edited version byte,\n   374\t   context flag, nonce byte, first body byte, last tag byte, wrong context, wrong key) were measured at\n   375\t   **148–205 µs** each over five consecutive runs, with a per-run spread of **1.05×–1.33×**, against the\n   376\t   < 4× bound the test enforces and its 40 µs floor (it prints the table on every run, so the number is\n   377\t   evidence in the build log, not a claim here). Note what the spread means: the tightest run was 1.05× and\n   378\t   the loosest 1.33×, which is ordinary noise, but it is also a reminder that the bound constrains a\n   379\t   *remote* timing signal and does not eliminate one — under load, GC or NUMA it will widen.\n   380\t   A caller that logs one failure path and not another, or that returns \"404\" for \"no such record\" but\n   381\t   \"403\" for \"cannot decrypt\", will leak far more than this code does.\n   382\t10. **Plaintext is yours.** `decrypt` returns a `byte[]`/`String` this component cannot erase after you\n   383\t    have used it, and `String` is not erasable in Java. If the plaintext outlives the call in a field, a\n   384\t    cache, a log line or an exception message, that is where the leak will be.\n   385\t11. **The IV/nonce is stored in the clear by design** (offset 3). Anyone who can read a record can see\n   386\t    which key version wrote it and its nonce. That is metadata GCM needs; it is why the version is\n   387\t    authenticated rather than trusted.\n   388\t12. **The context is authenticated, not confidential.** It travels in the text form as plaintext and it is\n   389\t    part of the AAD, which is exactly what makes it a routing key (\"tenant:42\"). Do not put anything in it\n   390\t    that must stay secret — a name, an ID you consider private, a PII fragment. If you need the *reason* for\n   391\t    a record to be private, encrypt it as plaintext and keep the context coarse.\n   392\t13. **`KeyVault` loads a key per operation, so a cheap call can become an expensive one.** Every cache miss\n   393\t    runs the source: a PBKDF2 miss costs ~462 ms of CPU, a keystore miss runs its own KDF, a file miss\n   394\t    touches the disk. That is the design (a cache full of live keys is the thing to avoid), but it means an\n   395\t    attacker who can make you miss can multiply your CPU by the miss rate. Put authentication and rate\n   396\t    limiting in front of `decryptStringEncoded`, and do not hand it unauthenticated input. `MAX_VERSIONS`\n   397\t    bounds how many distinct sources exist; it does not bound how often they are re-loaded.\n   398\t14. **The environment-variable key source is the weakest one.** Environment variables leak through\n   399\t    `/proc/<pid>/environ` (readable by the same uid, and by root), through crash handlers, through\n   400\t    `System.getenv()` dumps in diagnostics, and through anything that shells out with an inherited\n   401\t    environment. It exists because deployments already use it; a file with mode `0600`, a keystore, or a KMS\n   402\t    is strictly better. Nothing here can erase an env var.\n   403\t15. **`decrypt` attempts exactly one key version and does not fall back — that is a caller obligation, not\n   404\t    just a library behaviour.** If your storage legitimately holds records under a version you have dropped,\n   405\t    the record fails `KEY_UNAVAILABLE` and your code must not respond by \"trying the others\", because that\n   406\t    loop is the oracle this design removes. Plan retention into rotation: keep old versions readable until\n   407\t    the data under them is gone, then drop them deliberately.\n   408\t16. **The build pins versions but verifies no checksums.** `pom.xml` pins `junit-jupiter:5.10.2` and the\n   409\t    plugins used here, and the build runs offline against the local repository. There is no\n   410\t    dependency-management BOM, no `maven-dependency-plugin` hash check and no signature verification, so\n   411\t    \"pinned\" means \"whatever the local repository holds under that version\". Import the component into a\n   412\t    build that enforces artifact hashes or vendor the JDK-only sources; the component itself pulls nothing\n   413\t    at runtime, which is the only reason this is a low-severity gap.\n   414\t17. **The JVM keeps key copies this component cannot reach, and this is measured, not assumed.** Probes on\n   415\t    this JDK found that `PBKDF2KeyImpl` retains its own password and key arrays after `derive()` returns and\n   416\t    that its `destroy()` throws instead of clearing them; `SecretKeySpec` holds a clone of the bytes handed\n   417\t    to it. So \"we erased the key\" means \"we erased every copy we own\", and residual risk 8's caveats\n   418\t    (heap dumps, swap, core dumps) apply to the copies we cannot name.\n   419\t\n   420\t## Tests\n   421\t\n   422\t`mvn -o test` — 72 tests, ~7 s. They are written as attacks, not as smoke tests.\n   423\t\n   424\t| class | # | what it tries to do |\n   425\t|---|---|---|\n   426\t| `CipherBehaviourTest` | 27 | round-trips; nonce uniqueness across 500 records; every truncation length; bit flips in every byte; header relabelling and re-versioning through the public API; tag width (`theAuthenticationTagIsTheFullWidthTheFormatPromises`); one-record-one-spelling (`aRecordHasExactlyOneTextualSpelling`); timing parity across seven tamper routes; context validation; UTF-8 strictness; concurrency; `peekKeyVersion` correct on real records and `-1` on every junk shape **while staying free on an 8 MiB input**; These guards protect\n   334\t   against unprivileged users, other service accounts and configuration mistakes. They cannot protect a\n   335\t   key from a process that already owns the machine. (The suite prints this as a `NOTE` rather than\n   336\t   asserting it, and the ownership check cannot be exercised negatively while `user.name` is root.)\n   337\t2. **POSIX permissions are required.** On a filesystem without them (NTFS, FAT, some NFS/SMB mounts,\n   338\t   containers with odd mounts) `unix:mode` is unsupported and the component refuses to proceed — that is\n   339\t   a refusal, not a downgrade. The component cannot\n   343\t   see how many records a key has produced across processes, so `KeyVault.rotate()` is the control and\n   344\t   must be used routinely. If you will write more than ~10⁸ records under one key, rotate before you\n   345\t   get there.\n   346\t4. **`peekKeyVersion` reads attacker-chosen bytes.** Documented at the declaration; usable only as a\n   347\t   routing hint; the tag is the authority. A caller that treats it as authorisation reintroduces the\n   348\t   retargeting attack this format was designed to defeat. Because it is the one API that accepts a\n   349\t   record-shaped string *without* authenticating anything, it is also the natural place to aim a\n   350\t   resource-exhaustion or scan-cost attack, so its own work is bounded and measured: the length caps are\n   351\t   applied to the string before any decode, the decode touches only the first 4 characters (3 header\n   352\t   bytes) whatever the input size, a record too short to hold a header is refused on encoded length alone,\n   353\t   and every failure returns the same `-1` with no exception. The test feeds it 8 MiB and 64 MiB-shaped\n   354\t   junk and asserts the refusal costs nothing (`peek-oversized 0us` in the build log) as well as asserting\n   355\t   the correct version for real records, so the bound is not achieved by always answering `-1`.\n   356\t5. **PKCS#12's internal KDF is cheap.** A keystore protected by a human-chosen passphrase is a target.\n   357\t   `MIN_PASSPHRASE_CHARS = 32` is a floor, not a recommendation: use `RandomPasswords`, or a random\n   358\t   store password from a secret manager. The container is a *container*, not a lock.\n   359\t6. **`PasswordKeySource` protects an offline guess with 310 000 PBKDF2 iterations** (~462 ms of CPU per\n   360\t   attempt here). That is one guess's cost, not a rate limit. Attempt limiting and lockout belong to the\n   361\t   caller — and a leaked container file is a *file*, so an attacker can run the guesses in parallel on\n   362\t   their own hardware at whatever cost they can afford.\n   363\t7. **No key use limits, no revocation, no crypto-shredding.** Rotation stops *new* reads through an old\n   364\t   version by default, but old ciphertexts remain decryptable if you `enable` the old version — the\n   365\t   caller decides, explicitly. There is no per-key record counter and no audit log. `close()` is likewise\n   366\t   not a barrier: a `decrypt` already past its key load when `close()` runs is not waited for. It cannot\n   367\t   succeed with erased material — the derivation/unwrap or the tag then fails, so it errors out — but do\n   368\t   not treat `close()` as \"no operation is in flight\".
